# The rulings that move a figure, ranked (17 November 2026)

Energy Research Warehouse (ERW). Written by chain 402 (sessions 7900 on, the old laptop) for Samuel's review of 17 November 2026. Each entry is one question that waits for his ruling and that changes a figure a reader sees by 1 percent or more, or adds or removes a figure. Nothing here is decided and nothing was changed: no page, no default, no branch landed. A recommendation is not a ruling.

## How to read it

- **Three sections.** First the questions that change a headline figure: a number the page puts first (its headline tiles, its first sentence, a calculator's result as it opens), in any view the page's own menu reaches (a grid, a state, a month). Then the questions that change another figure (a table cell, a figure that depends on what the reader types). Then the questions that add or remove a figure.
- **Order inside a section:** largest change first. The change is the largest percent the measurement gives for that question. A change in points of a share is ranked by its points. Where the other choice shows other figures and computes none differently (another year, another list), it is ranked as 100 percent. In the third section the order is by how many figures are added or removed.
- **Where each figure is from:** the measurement file of each tool (written on 17 November from a script's printout) and the record of the branch that built the other choice (its test pins the figure). Both are linked under each entry. A figure the two give differently is listed at the foot under "Figures that do not agree", and that entry carries no recommendation.
- **A recommendation** is keep (the page as it is), switch (the other choice) or "no basis to recommend". It rests only on a published figure, a ruling Samuel already made, his page standard, or the tool's own method note. Where none of these decides, it says what there is to weigh.
- **Screenshots** are taken by the desktop today, one of the other choice and one of the page as it is. An entry says "screenshot not taken yet" until its pictures are pushed.
- **Evidence (added on 19 November 2026 by chain 503).** Under each entry that said "no basis to recommend" there is now a block "Evidence": how the publishers a reader would trust make the same choice, what Samuel has already ruled on a like question, and what the tool's own method note says, then "Recommendation after the evidence". The first recommendation is left as it was written. Nothing was changed and no ruling was taken. The same entries on one sheet: [rulings for one sitting](https://github.com/SamuelEnrique/erw/blob/main/docs/reviews/rulings-one-sitting-2026-11-19.md).
- **To rule:** write after `Ruling:` under an entry. The questions that are not here are in [the defaults list](https://github.com/SamuelEnrique/erw/blob/main/docs/reviews/rulings-defaults-2026-11-17.md), which can be closed with one "defaults stand".

## The counts

- **129 entries.** 31 change a headline figure, 36 change another figure, 62 add or remove a figure.
- **Recommendations:** keep 15, switch 26, no basis to recommend 88.
- **After the evidence of 19 November 2026 (chain 503):** of the 88 entries that said "no basis to recommend", 66 have been looked up and 22 wait: keep 24, switch 22, still no basis to recommend 20. With the 41 that had a recommendation, the list now stands at keep 39, switch 48, no basis to recommend 42.
- All 40 measurement files were read. Four entries cover a question that the rulings list holds twice (the event pages' held figures, the held grids' figures, the exchange's price indices, the map's copy of two held tables). The lines of the measurement files that are not here (a change under 1 percent, no change today, or a change that could not be computed, adding no figure) are in the defaults list.

## Section 1: lines that change a headline figure of a page

### 1. Local impact (the forecast tool), New York: New York: all sales tax exempt, or only the machinery's share

- **Page:** Local impact (the forecast tool), New York, https://erw-flame.vercel.app/impact
- **Question:** For a plant in New York the tool treats all of the sales tax as exempt. New York's exemption is for generating machinery, and in the other states with a machinery exemption the tool exempts only the machinery's share of the purchases. Which should New York use?
- **Now:** 100 percent exempt: sales and use tax USD 0 for each of New York's default plants. "Taxes, present value": wind (200 MW) USD 4.17 million, solar (150 MW) USD 7.08 million, gas (1,227 MW) USD 23.55 million.
- **Other choice:** Wind 66.9 percent exempt, solar 67.2, gas 58.3, as the other states hold it. Sales and use tax USD 8.66 million (wind), 4.81 million (solar), 29.56 million (gas). "Taxes, present value": wind USD 12.83 million, solar 11.89 million, gas 52.14 million.
- **Change:** "Taxes, present value": wind +208 percent, solar +68 percent, gas +121 percent. Sales and use tax appears where USD 0 stood.
- **Branch:** [wip/alt-impact-2](https://github.com/SamuelEnrique/erw/tree/wip/alt-impact-2)
- **Screenshots:** screenshot not taken yet
- **Recommendation:** **switch**. The tool's method note states its own rule: a machinery exemption "exempts the generating machinery and not the construction materials", held as the machinery's share of purchases. New York's statute (Tax Law 1115(a)(12)) is such an exemption, and the page's own hover calls the 100 percent "an upper bound of the exemption".
- **Figures from:** [the measurement, Local impact](https://github.com/SamuelEnrique/erw/blob/main/docs/reviews/ruling-impact-impact-2026-11-17.md), [the record of the branches, Local impact](https://github.com/SamuelEnrique/erw/blob/main/docs/reviews/ruling-alternatives-impact-2026-11-17.md)

`Ruling:`

### 2. Grid, the Mix tab: The Mix tab's three lead figures at one hour

- **Page:** Grid, the Mix tab, https://erw-flame.vercel.app/grid?grid=pjm&view=mix
- **Question:** The first sentence of the Mix tab gives a grid's demand, its exchange with its neighbours and its carbon, each at its own newest hour. Should all three be given at one hour, the newest hour all three hold?
- **Now:** Each figure at its own newest hour, with that hour printed. Read on 17 November: demand of 15 November 23:00 UTC, the exchange of 15 November 04:00 UTC, carbon of 12 November 23:00 UTC. ERCOT a net import of 145 MW, CAISO a net import of 5,604 MW, SPP a net export of 1,544 MW, PJM a net export of 1,267 MW; ERCOT's demand 54,008 MW.
- **Other choice:** All three at 12 November 23:00 UTC, the hour of the carbon figure, 72 hours before the newest demand. ERCOT a net export of 20 MW, CAISO a net export of 675 MW, SPP a net import of 1,112 MW, PJM a net export of 2,716 MW; ERCOT's demand 54,234 MW. Carbon does not move.
- **Change:** The exchange turns from import to export or back for three grids (ERCOT, CAISO, SPP); PJM's export +1,449 MW, +114 percent; demand ERCOT +0.42 percent, CAISO +13.88 percent. Every figure of the sentence would be three days old where a newer hour is held.
- **Branch:** [wip/alt-grid-1](https://github.com/SamuelEnrique/erw/tree/wip/alt-grid-1)
- **Screenshots:** the other choice: [grid-1-1280.png](https://github.com/SamuelEnrique/erw/blob/main/docs/reviews/ruling-alternatives/grid-1-1280.png); the page today: [grid-1-default-1280.png](https://github.com/SamuelEnrique/erw/blob/main/docs/reviews/ruling-alternatives/grid-1-default-1280.png)
- **Recommendation:** **no basis to recommend**. No published figure, earlier ruling, page standard or method note decides between one hour for all three and the newest hour of each. To weigh: the page prints each figure's hour today, so no figure is mislabelled; the other choice makes the three comparable but shows demand three days old, because EIA publishes the exchange about two days late and carbon later still.
- **Figures from:** [the measurement, Grid](https://github.com/SamuelEnrique/erw/blob/main/docs/reviews/ruling-impact-grid-2026-11-17.md), [the record of the branches, Grid](https://github.com/SamuelEnrique/erw/blob/main/docs/reviews/ruling-alternatives-grid-2026-11-17.md)
- **Evidence (looked up on 19 November 2026):**
  - **Published practice:** Two publishers, and they agree: each figure is shown at its own newest time, with the time said, and none is held back to the slowest. EIA, Today in Energy, "EIA's Hourly Electric Grid Monitor provides timely data about electricity usage patterns" (6 April 2020, https://www.eia.gov/todayinenergy/detail.php?id=43295) and "Product Highlight: EIA's Hourly Electric Grid Monitor" (6 December 2022, https://www.eia.gov/todayinenergy/detail.php?id=54899): the monitor is updated every hour with the demand of the hour before, while generation and total interchange come "with a one-day lag" and the exchange between neighbouring areas two days late; EIA publishes each as it has it. CAISO, Today's Outlook (https://www.caiso.com/todays-outlook, the notes under the charts): current demand, the demand trend on 5-minute averages, the capacity of the next hour (every 15 minutes) and of the next four hours (every 60 minutes) each stand at their own interval under one "As of" line. EIA's "About" page of the monitor (https://www.eia.gov/electricity/gridmonitor/about) gave no text to the fetch (it is drawn by script): not read today.
  - **Samuel's own rulings on a like question:** The Grid rulings of 18 October 2026, as `docs/methods/grid_network.md` records them ("One page, four tabs, and the Ask panel", the ruling on the age of "now"): the page keeps its newest hour and says how old it is, against the snapshot's refresh and against the clock. The ruling states the age; it does not move the figures to an older common hour. Nothing on one hour for the three figures in `docs/state/rulings_2026-10-11.md`, `docs/state/rulings_2026-11-06.md`, the roadmap's "Rulings" or the page standard (searched: hour, newest, lag, Mix, now).
  - **The tool's own method note:** `docs/methods/grid_network.md`, "How far behind the newest hour is, and why" (session 247): the exchange is about two days behind because EIA publishes it so, demand "is not behind", and the page shows each grid's demand at its own newest hour. "The two-day lag, said under the chart" (session 266): the Mix tab's sentence states the lag of its exchange hour. The decisions table gives the tab's rule: the latest hour that holds the total generation, and demand's own latest hour.
- **Recommendation after the evidence:** **keep**. The method note states the rule (each figure at its own newest hour, its hour and its lag printed), Samuel's ruling of 18 October 2026 on the age of "now" goes the same way, and EIA and CAISO both show each figure at its own newest time.

`Ruling:`

### 3. Company database, a parent company's page: A parent's page sums its subsidiaries' megawatts

- **Page:** Company database, a parent company's page, https://erw-flame.vercel.app/companies/duke-energy
- **Question:** A parent company's page shows no megawatts, because the plants are held by its subsidiaries. Should the parent's page add up its listed subsidiaries' megawatts, and list them?
- **Now:** Each legal entity keeps its own figures. Duke Energy: 0 MW operated, 0 MW owned. Dominion Generation: the same.
- **Other choice:** Duke Energy: "Operating, with its subsidiaries" 38,147.3 MW and 543.8 MW owned, with a table of its 2 subsidiaries (Duke Energy Carolinas 23,868 and 464; Duke Energy Progress 14,279 and 80). Dominion Generation: 28,681.0 MW operating and 5,821.5 MW owned, 3 subsidiaries. The sums stand beside the company's own figures. No other parent's page moves: the files name 6 parents, and the other four's subsidiaries hold no megawatts. Constellation Energy Corporation stays at 0 until a fuller list of parents exists.
- **Change:** Duke Energy 0 to 38,147.3 MW operated; Dominion Generation 0 to 28,681.0 MW.
- **Branch:** [wip/alt-companies-1](https://github.com/SamuelEnrique/erw/tree/wip/alt-companies-1)
- **Screenshots:** the other choice: [companies-1-parent-summed-1280.png](https://github.com/SamuelEnrique/erw/blob/main/docs/reviews/ruling-alternatives/companies-1-parent-summed-1280.png); the page today: screenshot not taken yet
- **Recommendation:** **no basis to recommend**. No allowed ground decides it. To weigh: the megawatts are EIA's public figures and each subsidiary is listed, so every sum can be traced; but only 2 of 6 parents would show a sum today, and well-known parents (Constellation, Vistra) would still read 0 until the data machine builds the list of who owns whom, which may read as if they own nothing.
- **Figures from:** [the measurement, company database](https://github.com/SamuelEnrique/erw/blob/main/docs/reviews/ruling-impact-companies-2026-11-17.md), [the record of the branch, company database](https://github.com/SamuelEnrique/erw/blob/main/docs/reviews/ruling-alternatives-companies-2026-11-17.md)
- **Evidence (looked up on 19 November 2026):**
  - **Published practice:** The publishers make this choice both ways. EIA, the source of the page's megawatts, keeps each reporting company's figures and names no parent: the Form EIA-860 detailed data page (https://www.eia.gov/electricity/data/eia860/, the list of files) describes the Utility file as data by utility, and the Owner file (Schedule 4) as holding the owners of generators "with shared ownership" or owned whole by someone other than the operator; nothing on that page attributes a plant or its capacity to a parent or holding company. (The form's instructions are under EIA's `/survey` path, which its robots file disallows: not read today.) Global Energy Monitor's Global Energy Ownership Tracker (https://globalenergymonitor.org/projects/global-energy-ownership-tracker/, the project page) does roll assets up: it maps each level of the chain from the direct owner to the ultimate parent, reports every link "with the percentage of ownership", and computes an effective ownership from that whole tree. So a tracker that sums to a parent does it from a full ownership tree with shares; the federal source does not sum at all.
  - **Samuel's own rulings on a like question:** None found. Searched `docs/state/rulings_2026-10-11.md`, `docs/state/rulings_2026-11-06.md`, the "Rulings" of `docs/roadmap.md` and the page standard (rule 9 of the chain file of 16 November 2026) for parent, subsidiary, holding company and summing: no ruling on whose megawatts a company's page counts. The page standard's lines "every figure carries its source document" and "an upgrade drops nothing the page had" are met by both choices (the sum would stand beside the company's own figures, each subsidiary listed).
  - **The tool's own method note:** `docs/methods/companies.md` does not say whether a parent's page sums. Rule 7 says a parent is a second field, never in place of the name, known from three places only (the NRC's parent field, a deal party written "X's Y", the reviewed aliases). Under "What it cannot say" it lists corporate families: EIA's files name operating companies and not their holding companies, so American Electric Power's operating utilities are separate rows with no parent.
- **Recommendation after the evidence:** **no basis to recommend**. Looked for a rule of EIA's that attributes capacity to a parent (none on the Form 860 page), a ruling of Samuel's on summing to a parent (none), and a rule in the method note (it names the gap, not a rule); the one publisher that sums does so from a whole ownership tree, which the ERW holds for 2 of 6 parents, and that does not settle whether a partial sum should be shown.

`Ruling:`

### 4. Interconnection queue explorer, ERCOT: Texas: count plants running, not agreements signed

- **Page:** Interconnection queue explorer, ERCOT, https://erw-flame.vercel.app/explore/interconnection?queue=ercot
- **Question:** For Texas, the page's second headline counts a project as soon as its connection agreement is signed, built or not, and since this week says so in words ("Agreement signed"). Should the count itself change, to the projects ERCOT has approved to switch on?
- **Now:** "Agreement signed", by the year a project entered the queue. On the page's table (rebuilt 19 October 2026): 2018 31 projects (6,345 MW), 2022 115 (23,750 MW), 2023 118, 2024 92, 2025 55 (20,949 MW). ERCOT's newer queue file counts 32, 116, 117, 89 and 44 for the same years.
- **Other choice:** "Reached operation", counted by the day ERCOT approved the plant to synchronize: 2018 21 projects (4,474 MW), 2020 12, 2021 15, 2022 15 (2,407 MW), 2023 7, 2024 9, 2025 0. The signed agreements that have not switched on are counted as waiting. The change reaches the project map's Queues tab and every other reader of ERCOT's status.
- **Change:** 2018 -32 percent, 2020 -74 percent, 2021 -79 percent, 2022 -87 percent, 2023 -94 percent, 2025 -100 percent (20,949 MW to 0).
- **Branch:** [wip/alt-interconnection-1](https://github.com/SamuelEnrique/erw/tree/wip/alt-interconnection-1)
- **Screenshots:** screenshot not taken yet
- **Recommendation:** **switch**. A published figure checks it: Berkeley Lab counts 17 Texas projects that entered in 2022 as operating; the other choice gives 15, the page's count 115. The queue connector already reads operation this way for every pull since 16 November, so the other choice applies one reading to all the years.
- **Figures from:** [the measurement, interconnection explorer](https://github.com/SamuelEnrique/erw/blob/main/docs/reviews/ruling-impact-interconnection-2026-11-17.md), [the record of the branches, interconnection explorer](https://github.com/SamuelEnrique/erw/blob/main/docs/reviews/ruling-alternatives-interconnection-2026-11-17.md)

`Ruling:`

### 5. Policy monitor, filtered by a state: Show PJM-wide actions under each PJM state

- **Page:** Policy monitor, filtered by a state, https://erw-flame.vercel.app/policy?view=monitor&state=VA
- **Question:** Filtering the monitor by Virginia shows the records that name Virginia, and not the actions of PJM, its grid operator, that bind every PJM state. Should a record that names PJM and no state appear under each PJM state?
- **Now:** Virginia: "7 shown of 1533 records held" (the view opens on the more significant records). A Grid filter stands beside the State filter.
- **Other choice:** Virginia: "14 shown of 1533". Each of PJM's 13 states and the District of Columbia gains the same 7 records: the District, Delaware and New Jersey 0 to 7; Indiana and Maryland 2 to 9; Michigan, North Carolina and West Virginia 3 to 10; Ohio 4 to 11; Illinois 6 to 13; Pennsylvania and Tennessee 7 to 14; Kentucky 8 to 15. With every record shown, Virginia 27 to 35. The list, the calendar and the download follow.
- **Change:** Virginia +7 records, +100 percent; with every record shown +29.6 percent.
- **Branch:** [wip/alt-policy-1](https://github.com/SamuelEnrique/erw/tree/wip/alt-policy-1)
- **Screenshots:** the other choice: [policy-1-pjm-wide-virginia-1280.png](https://github.com/SamuelEnrique/erw/blob/main/docs/reviews/ruling-alternatives/policy-1-pjm-wide-virginia-1280.png); the page today: screenshot not taken yet
- **Recommendation:** **no basis to recommend**. No allowed ground decides what a state filter should hold. To weigh: five of the fourteen (Michigan, Illinois, Kentucky, Tennessee, North Carolina) are in PJM only in part, so a PJM-wide action would be listed for places it does not reach; the Grid filter already gives the PJM records; and whether a record that names PJM and one state is PJM-wide is part of the ruling.
- **Figures from:** [the measurement, policy monitor](https://github.com/SamuelEnrique/erw/blob/main/docs/reviews/ruling-impact-policy-2026-11-17.md), [the record of the branches, policy monitor](https://github.com/SamuelEnrique/erw/blob/main/docs/reviews/ruling-alternatives-policy-2026-11-17.md)
- **Evidence (looked up on 19 November 2026):**
  - **Published practice:** No publisher was found that makes this choice. Looked for a public policy tracker with a state filter that says where a grid operator's region-wide action is listed. The National Conference of State Legislatures was not read (its robots file disallows this reader on every path), and the state list of DSIRE, the North Carolina Clean Energy Technology Center's policy database (https://programs.dsireusa.org/system/program/va), did not draw its rows for a reader without a browser: not read today. The Federal Register and the agencies whose releases the monitor holds offer no state filter of their own. So published practice does not decide it.
  - **Samuel's own rulings on a like question:** None found. Searched `docs/state/rulings_2026-10-11.md`, `docs/state/rulings_2026-11-06.md`, the "Rulings" of `docs/roadmap.md` and the page standard (rule 9 of the chain file of 16 November 2026) for filter, PJM, grid operator, region and state: no ruling on what a state filter holds.
  - **The tool's own method note:** `docs/methods/policy_monitor.md`, section 18, states the rule as the page tells it to the reader: the State filter's hover says a record is a federal action or a release of one of four bodies, and "a grid operator's action that names no state is under Grid" (written by the read of 16 November 2026, session 7204). The Grid filter stands beside the State filter, so the seven PJM-wide records are one click away under PJM.
- **Recommendation after the evidence:** **keep**. The method note decided it: it states, and the page's own hover tells the reader, that a grid operator's action naming no state is listed under Grid and not under a state; no publisher and no ruling says otherwise, and five of the fourteen PJM places are in PJM only in part.

`Ruling:`

### 6. Shoulder hours, Texas by month: Look for the midday low only in daylight

- **Page:** Shoulder hours, Texas by month, https://erw-flame.vercel.app/shoulder?grid=ercot
- **Question:** The third headline, "The midday surplus", is measured around the lowest hour of the whole day, which in Texas was often at night before solar grew. Should the page look for that low only while the sun is up?
- **Now:** The whole day's lowest hour. Texas: February 2019 29,972 MWh of midday surplus, December 2021 31,661 MWh, January 2023 22,918 MWh, February 2023 11,508 MWh, November 2023 17,933 MWh. In 60 of the 93 Texas months held the low is between 01:00 and 04:00.
- **Other choice:** The lowest hour while the sun is up (solar at least 5 percent of its highest hour on the month's average day). The surplus changes in 22 Texas months, January 2019 to November 2023: 7 fall to 0 MWh (February 2019 and December 2021 among them; the measurement file's summary says 8, its own printout and the branch's test 7); January 2023 5,852 MWh; November 2023 8,925 MWh; February 2023 12,014 MWh. In the other 38 months whose low moves, the hours and MWh stay the same. No month after May 2024 and no California month moves.
- **Change:** -100 percent in 7 months; -24 to -99 percent in most of the others (January 2023 -74.5 percent, November 2023 -50.2 percent); one month rises (February 2023 +4.4 percent). The page as it opens does not move.
- **Branch:** [wip/alt-shoulder-1](https://github.com/SamuelEnrique/erw/tree/wip/alt-shoulder-1). The page draws rows of a table, so the change shows only after the data machine rebuilds the table with the branch's builder.
- **Screenshots:** screenshot not taken yet
- **Recommendation:** **no basis to recommend**. The method note defines the midday surplus around "net load's lowest hour" and names no time of day, so it does not decide. To weigh: the figure is called midday and in 60 Texas months it is taken at night; the other choice changes only months up to November 2023; the 5 percent threshold for "the sun is up" is itself a choice.
- **Figures from:** [the measurement, shoulder hours](https://github.com/SamuelEnrique/erw/blob/main/docs/reviews/ruling-impact-shoulder-2026-11-17.md), [the record of the branches, shoulder hours](https://github.com/SamuelEnrique/erw/blob/main/docs/reviews/ruling-alternatives-shoulder-2026-11-17.md)
- **Evidence (looked up on 19 November 2026):**
  - **Published practice:** Three publishers read, and all three use midday for the hours of solar output; none takes a midday figure from the night. EIA, Today in Energy, "As solar capacity grows, duck curves are getting deeper in California" (21 June 2023, https://www.eia.gov/todayinenergy/detail.php?id=56880): net load is demand less variable renewable output, it falls lowest in the middle of the day when solar output tends to be highest, and EIA names that low "the midday dip in the net load curve". CAISO's Department of Market Monitoring, *2024 Special Report on Battery Storage* (29 May 2025, https://www.caiso.com/documents/2024-special-report-on-battery-storage-may-29-2025.pdf, page 3, PDF page 4): its "peak solar hours" are hours-ending 10 to 13, when the batteries charge; its *2025 Annual Report on Market Issues and Performance* (June 2026, https://www.caiso.com/documents/2025-annual-report-on-market-issues-and-performance.pdf, page 2) says the same of the mid-day hours. Potomac Economics, *2025 State of the Market Report for the ERCOT Electricity Markets* (June 2026, https://www.potomaceconomics.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/06/2025-State-of-the-Market-Report-for-ERCOT.pdf, page 6): the middle of the day is the hours when solar production peaks, "between 9 a.m. and 2 p.m."; page 13 has solar most likely to be curtailed at midday, and page 23 ties the fall of real-time prices to solar output in the midday hours. The page's own lows agree with that window where solar leads (09:00 to 14:00 in every California month, and in Texas since July 2024). None of the three publishes a midday surplus in MWh, and none gives a threshold for when the sun is up: the 5 percent of the other choice has no published source.
  - **Samuel's own rulings on a like question:** None found (searched `docs/state/rulings_2026-10-11.md`, `docs/state/rulings_2026-11-06.md`, the roadmap's "Rulings", the page standard and `CLAUDE.md` for shoulder, midday, surplus and daylight). The page standard (rule 9 of `queue/done/302-day-16-november-oldlaptop-large-load-on-the-site.md`, 16 November 2026) asks that the tool state facts under plain names; it does not say at which hour the low is taken.
  - **The tool's own method note:** `docs/methods/shoulder_hours.md`, "On the average day": the midday surplus is the run of hours around net load's lowest hour in which net load is below its daily mean; no time of day is named, and the note's opening question is about solar flooding the middle of the day. `docs/methods/shoulder.md`, section 5 (key `surplus`): the lowest hour of the whole day wherever it falls, between 01:00 and 04:00 in 60 of Texas's 93 months. Section 10, item 4, says in its own words that in those months the figure is a run from the small hours into the morning, keeps the rule and the name as the default taken, and leaves a rule that looks only while the sun is up to the owner's word. So the note records the present rule and does not settle it.
- **Recommendation after the evidence:** **switch**. Published practice decided it: EIA and both grids' market monitors use midday for the hours of solar output, and the page's own note says the figure of 60 Texas months is taken in the night; the 5 percent threshold for "the sun is up" has no published source and stays part of the ruling.

`Ruling:`

### 7. Price board, the strip at the top: The strip's highest and lowest from daily series only

- **Page:** Price board, the strip at the top, https://erw-flame.vercel.app/board
- **Question:** The strip at the top names the series highest and the series lowest in its own one-year range. A monthly average can stand there beside daily prices. Should the two be chosen from daily series only?
- **Now:** From every series (30 candidates on the file of 17 November). Highest: the uranium price, October 2026's monthly average, 72.89, at the very top of its year's range. Lowest: the cobalt price, October's average, 36,288.27, at the very bottom of its range.
- **Other choice:** From daily series only (21 candidates). Highest: the 2-year Treasury yield, 4.86 percent on 12 November. Lowest: the Henry Hub gas price, USD 3.00 on 11 November.
- **Change:** Both cells of the strip name another series. No figure is computed differently.
- **Branch:** [wip/alt-board-4](https://github.com/SamuelEnrique/erw/tree/wip/alt-board-4)
- **Screenshots:** screenshot not taken yet
- **Recommendation:** **no basis to recommend**. The board's method note defines each row's one-year range and does not say which rows the strip may pick from. To weigh: a monthly average is weeks old when it leads the strip beside prices of this week; but uranium and cobalt have no daily series on the board, so they could never appear there.
- **Figures from:** [the measurement, price board](https://github.com/SamuelEnrique/erw/blob/main/docs/reviews/ruling-impact-board-2026-11-17.md), [the record of the branches, price board](https://github.com/SamuelEnrique/erw/blob/main/docs/reviews/ruling-alternatives-board-2026-11-17.md)
- **Evidence (looked up on 19 November 2026):**
  - **Published practice:** One publisher read, and it does not make this choice. EIA, "Today in Energy: Daily Prices" (https://www.eia.gov/todayinenergy/prices.php, the page of 18 November 2026, all five tables): every figure is one trading day's close or settlement, no monthly average stands among them, and the page has no cell for the highest or lowest series in a year's range; its one comparison is the percent change from the business day before. The exchanges' own pages were not read today (ICE's terms of use bar robots and data gathering; CME's site did not answer), so how an exchange counts a 52-week range is not read today and decides nothing here.
  - **Samuel's own rulings on a like question:** None found on which rows the strip may pick from (searched `docs/state/rulings_2026-10-11.md`, `docs/state/rulings_2026-11-06.md`, the roadmap's "Rulings" and the page standard for strip, range, monthly and daily). The nearest: his ruling of 18 October 2026, as `docs/methods/price_board.md` records it (decision 5 of the page's list), gives a monthly row 132 days before it is stale against 10 for a daily one, so by his own rule October's monthly average is a current value on 17 November, not a stale one.
  - **The tool's own method note:** `docs/methods/price_board.md`, "The headline strip and the week table, as a trader reads them" (session 7538, 16 November 2026): the two range cells take the benchmarks of every step, so a monthly average can stand there, and each cell now gives its value's date and its stale mark. The same sentence marks this as row 15 of the board's read (`docs/reviews/board-read-2026-11-16.md`), a default kept until this ruling, so the note records the present rule and does not settle it. (The ranked list says the note is silent on the strip's candidates; since session 7538 it is not.)
- **Recommendation after the evidence:** **no basis to recommend**. EIA's daily price page keeps to daily closes but has no such cell, the exchanges' definitions could not be read, no ruling of Samuel's touches the strip, and the method note carries the present candidates only as a default that waits for this ruling.

`Ruling:`

### 8. Interconnection queue explorer: Open on a year with tens of projects, not 4

- **Page:** Interconnection queue explorer, https://erw-flame.vercel.app/explore/interconnection
- **Question:** The page opens on California's newest complete year, 2023, which holds 4 projects. Should it open on a year with tens of projects?
- **Now:** As it opens (California, entry year 2023): 4 projects entered (664 MW), 0 reached operation, withdrawn share 1.00, 0 waiting.
- **Other choice:** California, entry year 2021, the newest complete year with 20 or more projects: 361 projects entered (105,537 MW), 2 reached operation, withdrawn share 0.71, 104 waiting. Texas and the Southwest Power Pool open on 2025 either way (354 and 421 projects).
- **Change:** Every headline of the opening view shows another year's figures: 4 projects to 361, withdrawn share 1.00 to 0.71. No figure is computed differently.
- **Branch:** [wip/alt-interconnection-2](https://github.com/SamuelEnrique/erw/tree/wip/alt-interconnection-2)
- **Screenshots:** the other choice: [interconnection-2-1280.png](https://github.com/SamuelEnrique/erw/blob/main/docs/reviews/ruling-alternatives/interconnection-2-1280.png); the page today: [interconnection-2-default-1280.png](https://github.com/SamuelEnrique/erw/blob/main/docs/reviews/ruling-alternatives/interconnection-2-default-1280.png)
- **Recommendation:** **no basis to recommend**. Which year a page opens on is not settled by a published figure, an earlier ruling, the page standard or the method note. To weigh: the opening view now rests on 4 projects, all withdrawn, so its first three numbers read 4, 0 and 1.00; the other choice opens two years further back; the 20-project floor is itself a choice.
- **Figures from:** [the measurement, interconnection explorer](https://github.com/SamuelEnrique/erw/blob/main/docs/reviews/ruling-impact-interconnection-2026-11-17.md), [the record of the branches, interconnection explorer](https://github.com/SamuelEnrique/erw/blob/main/docs/reviews/ruling-alternatives-interconnection-2026-11-17.md)
- **Evidence (looked up on 19 November 2026):**
  - **Published practice:** Berkeley Lab, "Queued Up: 2026 Edition" (the slide deck, https://eta-publications.lbl.gov/sites/default/files/2026-06/queued_up_2026_edition.pdf), does not give an outcome share for a recent or thin entry year. Slide 35 gives the shares withdrawn and operating only for requests that entered from 2000 to 2020, 21,927 of them, because the outcome of recent years "may not yet be determined"; slide 51 prints the number of projects behind each region's wait by year, and slide 42 warns where a year's sample is small. It names no opening year and no floor of 20. No grid operator's own queue report was read today, and Berkeley Lab's landing page on emp.lbl.gov answered 403 and was not read. So the publisher shows shares only on full, settled groups, and does not rule on a page's first view.
  - **Samuel's own rulings on a like question:** "New York shows day-ahead first", 12 October 2026 (`docs/roadmap.md`, "Rulings"). It answered item G3 of `docs/state/rulings_2026-10-11.md`: New York's real-time history lacked 19 days, so the page opens on the series that is whole. Here California's 2023 holds 4 of the requests made that year (Berkeley Lab counts 594), so the like choice is to open on the newest year that is whole. No ruling names the interconnection explorer itself (searched the two rulings files, the roadmap's rulings and the page standard for opens, default, year and queue).
  - **The tool's own method note:** The two notes of the queue pages differ. `docs/methods/interconnection_explorer.md`, "The filters": the last entry year opens on "the newest year that is over", and "What a queue does not hold" says the opening headline (4 entered, share 1.00) is true of California's report "and far from the requests made". `docs/methods/interconnection_queue_summary.md`, "When a figure is not written" (the rule of `/queues`): a share is written only from 20 requests or more, since under that one project moves it by five points or more.
- **Recommendation after the evidence:** **switch**. Samuel's ruling of 12 October 2026 that New York opens on the series that is whole decided it, and the floor of 20 is the site's own: `/queues` already writes no share from fewer than 20 requests.

At 2 hours the storm day reads 203.31; at 8 hours 398.07 and the negative-price day 1.54. Every player's score moves with it, and scores already on the leaderboard would no longer compare.

### 12. Shoulder hours: Which shoulder measure is the headline

- **Page:** Shoulder hours, https://erw-flame.vercel.app/shoulder
- **Question:** The page has two measures of the evening shoulder. Which one should be the headline: the energy above the day's average net load (cut off at midnight), or the second measure, the energy above the midpoint of the evening peak?
- **Now:** The first measure, against the day's average. Texas, August 2026: a shoulder of 5 hours and 66,630 MWh, 3.66 hours of the battery fleet needed; the mean of 2025 4.21 hours; 11 months of 2025 cut at midnight. California by EIA's data, November 2025: 8 hours, 2.38 hours needed. California by its grid operator's own data, August 2026: 4.06.
- **Other choice:** The second measure as the headline; the first stays lower on the page. Texas, August 2026: 3 hours (19:00 to 22:00) and 22,526 MWh, 1.24 hours needed; the mean of 2025 1.37; no month cut at midnight. California by EIA's data, November 2025: 7 hours, 0.78. By the grid operator's own data, August 2026: 1.54.
- **Change:** Hours needed, Texas August 2026 -2.42, -66.2 percent; the 2025 mean -67.5 percent; California -67.2 percent and -62.2 percent. The second measure is the smaller in every month.
- **Branch:** [wip/alt-shoulder-2](https://github.com/SamuelEnrique/erw/tree/wip/alt-shoulder-2)
- **Screenshots:** screenshot not taken yet
- **Recommendation:** **no basis to recommend**. Both measures are defined in the method note and neither is checked against a published figure. To weigh: the first measure is cut at midnight in 11 of 2025's 12 Texas months, so it understates a shoulder that runs past midnight; the second counts only energy above a higher level and reads about a third of the first.
- **Figures from:** [the measurement, shoulder hours](https://github.com/SamuelEnrique/erw/blob/main/docs/reviews/ruling-impact-shoulder-2026-11-17.md), [the record of the branches, shoulder hours](https://github.com/SamuelEnrique/erw/blob/main/docs/reviews/ruling-alternatives-shoulder-2026-11-17.md)
- **Evidence (looked up on 19 November 2026):**
  - **Published practice:** The publishers do not make this choice: none measures a shoulder, in hours or in MWh, against any level. What they do publish is the evening need as clock hours around the peak of net load, and their hours differ. Potomac Economics, *2025 State of the Market Report for the ERCOT Electricity Markets* (June 2026, https://www.potomaceconomics.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/06/2025-State-of-the-Market-Report-for-ERCOT.pdf, page 5): the net load peak, demand less renewable output, falls in the later evening hours, "7 to 9 p.m.". CAISO's Department of Market Monitoring, *2024 Special Report on Battery Storage* (29 May 2025, https://www.caiso.com/documents/2024-special-report-on-battery-storage-may-29-2025.pdf, page 30, PDF page 31): its peak net load hours are hours 17 to 22; its *2025 Annual Report on Market Issues and Performance* (June 2026, https://www.caiso.com/documents/2025-annual-report-on-market-issues-and-performance.pdf, pages 35 and 43) names 4 to 9 p.m. and has the average net load peak in hour-ending 20. The National Renewable Energy Laboratory (Denholm, Nunemaker, Gagnon and Cole, *The Potential for Battery Energy Storage to Provide Peaking Capacity in the United States*, NREL/TP-6A20-74184, June 2019, https://docs.nlr.gov/docs/fy19osti/74184.pdf, PDF pages 10 to 13) sizes storage against the top of the net demand peak: how much storage of a duration can lower the net peak by its full power, which it calls the "peak demand reduction credit". In kind all of these stand around the peak, which is nearer the second measure than the first (energy above the day's mean, cut at midnight); in Texas, August 2026, the second measure's 19:00 to 22:00 sits on Potomac's hours. But none prints either of the page's measures, none uses the midpoint between the mean and the peak, and their windows run from 2 hours to 6.
  - **Samuel's own rulings on a like question:** None found (searched `docs/state/rulings_2026-10-11.md`, `docs/state/rulings_2026-11-06.md`, the roadmap's "Rulings" and the page standard for shoulder, measure, headline, midnight and level). The page standard (16 November 2026) says an upgrade drops nothing the page had; both measures stay on the page under either choice, so it does not pick the headline.
  - **The tool's own method note:** `docs/methods/shoulder_hours.md`, "What it cannot see": the shoulder against the daily mean ends at midnight in California and, since 2025, in Texas; a shoulder against a higher level would be shorter; and the note says this is "a definition for Samuel to rule on". The same note calls the second measure one meant to end inside the evening (session 80). `docs/methods/shoulder.md`, section 10, item 2: default taken, both measures stay and the first is the headline; section 9: nothing published stands against the shoulder's length or the hours needed, since the term and the measure are the page's own.
- **Recommendation after the evidence:** **no basis to recommend**. Looked for a published measure of the evening stretch's length or energy and found none: the two market monitors and the laboratory frame the evening around the net load peak, nearer the second measure in kind, but with windows of 2 to 6 hours and no measure like either of the page's, and the method note leaves the level to Samuel by name.

`Ruling:`

### 13. What a battery saves a customer: Cap the energy shifted at what the building can use

- **Page:** What a battery saves a customer, https://erw-flame.vercel.app/battery/customer
- **Question:** The energy a battery moves out of the peak-rate hours is not limited by how much the building uses in those hours. Should it be capped at the building's peak demand times the peak hours?
- **Now:** Not capped. On every published tariff case the cap would not bind. On an oversized battery (a building with a 50 kW peak, a 200 kW and 800 kWh battery): "From shifting energy" USD 2,008.47 a month, "Saved a year" USD 34,901.65.
- **Other choice:** Capped at the peak kW times the peak hours (200 kWh a day in that example): "From shifting energy" USD 502.12 a month, "Saved a year" USD 16,825.41. No published case moves; the page says when it caps and by how much.
- **Change:** Published cases: 0. The oversized example: -75.0 percent from shifting energy, -51.8 percent a year.
- **Branch:** [wip/alt-battery-customer-4](https://github.com/SamuelEnrique/erw/tree/wip/alt-battery-customer-4)
- **Screenshots:** screenshot not taken yet
- **Recommendation:** **no basis to recommend**. No published case moves, so no published figure checks it, and no ruling or method rule covers an oversized battery. To weigh: without a cap the page can credit a battery for moving more energy than the building uses; the cap uses the building's peak as an upper bound, and a true cap would need a new field for the building's use in those hours.
- **Figures from:** [the measurement, customer battery](https://github.com/SamuelEnrique/erw/blob/main/docs/reviews/ruling-impact-battery-customer-2026-11-17.md), [the record of the branches, customer battery](https://github.com/SamuelEnrique/erw/blob/main/docs/reviews/ruling-alternatives-battery-customer-2026-11-17.md)
- **Evidence (looked up on 19 November 2026):**
  - **Published practice:** One publisher read, in two documents, and in both the battery's energy is always inside what the building draws. National Renewable Energy Laboratory, Neubauer and Simpson, *Deployment of Behind-The-Meter Energy Storage for Demand Charge Reduction* (NREL/TP-5400-63162, January 2015, https://docs.nlr.gov/docs/fy15osti/63162.pdf): the battery is run by a peak-shaving controller on the measured loads of 98 buildings, so it discharges only to lower the building's own load; page 9 calls a cycle every day a worst case for the energy used, and page 17 says the effect on energy cost is "much smaller than the effects on demand charges". The same laboratory's DiOrio, Dobos and Janzou, *Economic Analysis Case Studies of Battery Energy Storage with SAM* (NREL/TP-6A20-64987, November 2015, https://docs.nlr.gov/docs/fy16osti/64987.pdf, PDF page 9): its controller first computes the grid power the building needs without a battery at every step, then discharges to lower the highest of those. Neither credits a battery for energy the building does not use. A utility's tariff was not read today for what it pays for energy sent back through the meter, so no tariff decides anything here; Lazard, the Annual Technology Baseline and EIA do not model a customer's bill.
  - **Samuel's own rulings on a like question:** None found on a cap or on an oversized battery (searched `docs/state/rulings_2026-10-11.md`, `docs/state/rulings_2026-11-06.md`, the roadmap's "Rulings" and the page standard for customer, cap, shift and oversized). The page standard (16 November 2026) says real numbers only; it names no rule for a typed case.
  - **The tool's own method note:** `docs/methods/battery_customer.md`, section 6, "A battery larger than the load": the rule moves the whole battery every day whatever the building uses in the peak-rate hours, and the note says in its own words that a battery holding more than the building uses in those hours could not discharge it all behind the meter; it gives as the only reason for no cap that the tool does not know the building's use (fault row 4). Section 3, rule 1, already caps what comes off the peak at the peak itself, so the page's two parts follow two rules. Section 5 calls one full cycle a day a choice of session 88 and cites the laboratory's worst case. The other choice needs no new field: it caps at the peak kW times the hours typed, an upper bound on the use, and moves none of the nine published tariff cases.
- **Recommendation after the evidence:** **switch**. The tool's own method note decided it: it says a battery cannot discharge behind the meter more than the building uses in those hours, which is what the uncapped figure credits, and the laboratory's two models agree; the cap at the peak times the hours is an upper bound, so a figure can still be too high, never lower than the true one.

`Ruling:`

### 14. The deal tracker: Open the deal tracker at a confidence floor

- **Page:** The deal tracker, https://erw-flame.vercel.app/deals
- **Question:** Each deal is read from news by a model, which gives its own confidence in the reading. The tracker shows every deal from a confidence of 0.3 up. Should it open at a floor of 0.5 or 0.7, as the Sunday Roundup's tables use 0.7? (The tracker is held for a license today, so no visitor sees these counts either way.)
- **Now:** No floor: 578 deals, 40,297 MW stated by 35 of them, 212 deals for AI power (on the repository's copy of the table; the live site holds a few more).
- **Other choice:** At 0.7: 281 deals, 21,705 MW, 99 deals for AI power. At 0.5: 525 deals, all the megawatts kept, 201 for AI power.
- **Change:** At 0.7: deals -51.4 percent, megawatts -46.1 percent, AI-power deals -53.3 percent. At 0.5: deals -9.2 percent.
- **Branch:** [wip/alt-deals-1](https://github.com/SamuelEnrique/erw/tree/wip/alt-deals-1). The branch takes 0.7.
- **Screenshots:** the other choice: [deals-1-floor-held-1280.png](https://github.com/SamuelEnrique/erw/blob/main/docs/reviews/ruling-alternatives/deals-1-floor-held-1280.png); the page today: screenshot not taken yet
- **Recommendation:** **no basis to recommend**. No allowed ground sets the floor. To weigh: the Roundup already uses 0.7, so the newsletter and the tracker count differently; at 0.7 half the deals and nearly half the megawatts leave the opening view, though a reader could still lower the floor.
- **Figures from:** [the measurement, deal tracker](https://github.com/SamuelEnrique/erw/blob/main/docs/reviews/ruling-impact-deals-2026-11-17.md), [the record of the branches, deal tracker](https://github.com/SamuelEnrique/erw/blob/main/docs/reviews/ruling-alternatives-deals-2026-11-17.md)
- **Evidence (looked up on 19 November 2026):**
  - **Published practice:** No public tracker reads its deals with a model, so none publishes a confidence floor; the two read set a bar of evidence before a row is counted. The Clean Energy Buyers Association's Deal Tracker (https://ceba.org/deal-tracker/, the inclusion criteria) counts a deal only where it is "publicly announced" or can be reached in the public domain or a regulatory filing. The Clean Investment Monitor (https://www.cleaninvestmentmonitor.org/methodology, "How we categorize investment") lists a project as announced only once its place and timeline are named, and its headline counts money spent, not announcements. Both would leave out a row that is doubtful to be a transaction; neither says where a score from 0 to 1 should be cut.
  - **Samuel's own rulings on a like question:** None found on a floor. Searched `docs/state/rulings_2026-10-11.md`, `docs/state/rulings_2026-11-06.md`, the "Rulings" of `docs/roadmap.md` and the page standard for confidence, floor, sure and deal. The Roundup's 0.7 is a constant of the code (`ROW_CONFIDENCE` in `warehouse/news/roundup.py`, described in `docs/methods/roundup_selection.md`), not a ruling. The page standard's line that an upgrade "drops nothing the page had" bears on it only in part: under a floor the rows stay in the table and the reader can lower it.
  - **The tool's own method note:** `docs/methods/deal_tracker.md` leaves it open by name. Section 7.1: "Sure" is the extraction's own confidence, 0.8 or more for a stated deal between named parties, 0.6 to 0.8 for a stated deal with a field unclear, under 0.6 where it is doubtful that there is a transaction; the table shows every row. Decision 10 and section 12, item 2: whether the table opens at a floor is the owner's; default no floor. Section 2 defines a deal as a specific transaction between named parties.
- **Recommendation after the evidence:** **no basis to recommend**. The published trackers and the note's own bands both point to leaving out rows that are doubtful to be a transaction, but the edge of that band is 0.6, which is neither built choice (0.5 or 0.7), and nothing found says which value Samuel's tracker should take; the note itself names the floor as his call.

`Ruling:`

### 16. The home battery game: Score net of the free starting charge

- **Page:** The home battery game, https://erw-flame.vercel.app/play/battery
- **Question:** The battery starts each day half full, and that starting energy is free. Should the score be counted net of the starting charge, valued at the day's first price?
- **Now:** The start is free (the game's rule since it was first built). The best possible score on Normal: the storm day USD 240.23, the calm day 0.44, the solar day 0.93, the heat day 60.97, the negative-price day 0.79.
- **Other choice:** Net of the start (6.75 kWh at the first 15 minutes' price): the storm day USD 181.88 (the start is worth 58.35 at USD 8,644.11 per MWh), the calm day 0.26, the solar day 0.77, the heat day 60.78, the negative-price day 1.01 (the start is worth less than nothing at a price of -31.57). A play with no move scores minus the start (the storm day -58.35).
- **Change:** The storm day -24.3 percent, the calm day -41.4 percent, the solar day -17.0 percent, the heat day -0.3 percent, the negative-price day +26.8 percent.
- **Branch:** [wip/alt-play-battery-2](https://github.com/SamuelEnrique/erw/tree/wip/alt-play-battery-2)
- **Screenshots:** screenshot not taken yet
- **Recommendation:** **no basis to recommend**. No allowed ground decides how a game should score. To weigh: which price values the start is part of the ruling (the rulings list's own USD 45.66 for the storm day uses another price than the first 15 minutes'); a player who does nothing would score below zero; past scores would no longer compare.
- **Figures from:** [the measurement, home battery game](https://github.com/SamuelEnrique/erw/blob/main/docs/reviews/ruling-impact-play-battery-2026-11-17.md), [the record of the branches, home battery game](https://github.com/SamuelEnrique/erw/blob/main/docs/reviews/ruling-alternatives-play-battery-2026-11-17.md)
- **Evidence (looked up on 19 November 2026):**
  - **Published practice:** The publishers do not make this choice: none scores a game. In their own accounts of a battery the stored energy is never free, but none says at which price a starting charge is valued. Lazard, *Levelized Cost of Energy+* (2026 edition, https://www.lazard.com/media/kcfconhf/lazards-lcoeplus_vf.pdf, PDF page 38, the storage key assumptions): every MWh its storage cases discharge is bought at a charging cost of USD 40 per MWh. Potomac Economics, *2025 State of the Market Report for the ERCOT Electricity Markets* (June 2026, https://www.potomaceconomics.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/06/2025-State-of-the-Market-Report-for-ERCOT.pdf, page 23): the fleet's energy revenue is given net, "after accounting for charging costs". CAISO's Department of Market Monitoring, *2025 Annual Report on Market Issues and Performance* (June 2026, https://www.caiso.com/documents/2025-annual-report-on-market-issues-and-performance.pdf, pages 118 and 119): its model battery's charge is held between 0 and 400 MWh, and the report does not say what charge it starts with or how that charge is valued. These are cost and revenue accounts, not a game's score.
  - **Samuel's own rulings on a like question:** None found on the starting charge (searched `docs/state/rulings_2026-10-11.md`, `docs/state/rulings_2026-11-06.md`, the roadmap's "Rulings" and the page standard for game, score and start). His rulings of 14 October 2026 on this game, as `docs/methods/battery_game.md` ("Version 5") records them from `docs/reviews/games-2026-10-14/README.md`, changed the backup reserve, Hard's notice, the fleet of 10,000 homes and the modes; none of the questions put to him was the starting charge, and the note says the scorer was left as it was.
  - **The tool's own method note:** `docs/methods/battery_game.md`, "The fixed rules": the battery starts half full. "The end table": a sentence under the table tells the player that the battery starts half full, so a row can sell more than it bought. "Difficulties" and "The leaderboard": Normal with the default battery scores as the first game did (a test holds it), and plays are ranked only against the same rules version, so a new way of scoring would start new boards. The note's newer copy on the branch `wip/7535-play-battery` (not landed) lists a score net of the starting charge among what the game does not hold, as a ruling that waits. So the note records the present rule and marks the question open.
- **Recommendation after the evidence:** **no basis to recommend**. Looked for a publisher's rule on valuing a battery's starting charge and found none (Lazard and the two market monitors count charging as a cost but score no game and name no price for a start), no ruling of Samuel's touches the start, and the method note holds the free start as a fixed rule while its newest copy lists the net score as waiting for him.

`Ruling:`

### 17. Local impact (the forecast tool), Washington geothermal: Washington geothermal: at cost, or the state's steam plant schedule

- **Page:** Local impact (the forecast tool), Washington geothermal, https://erw-flame.vercel.app/impact
- **Question:** A geothermal plant in Washington is valued for property tax at its full cost every year. Should it follow the depreciation schedule Washington uses for steam and gas fired plants?
- **Now:** At cost. Washington geothermal as it opens (the state's average levy, 8.96 per 1,000): property tax over the life USD 80.89 million, first year 2.02 million; "Taxes, present value" USD 25.20 million.
- **Other choice:** The state's steam and gas fired schedule (92.5 percent of cost at age 1, down to a floor of 15 percent): over the life USD 34.66 million, first year 1.87 million; "Taxes, present value" USD 15.48 million.
- **Change:** "Taxes, present value" -38.6 percent; property tax over the life -57.2 percent.
- **Branch:** [wip/alt-impact-12](https://github.com/SamuelEnrique/erw/tree/wip/alt-impact-12)
- **Screenshots:** screenshot not taken yet
- **Recommendation:** **no basis to recommend**. The method note's recorded ruling is that where no schedule was read for a plant, its value is held at cost, an upper bound, and said so. Whether the steam and gas fired schedule is Washington's schedule for a geothermal plant has not been read, so that ruling does not settle it. To weigh: at cost overstates a 30-year-old plant's tax; the schedule is the state's own but named for another kind of plant.
- **Figures from:** [the measurement, Local impact](https://github.com/SamuelEnrique/erw/blob/main/docs/reviews/ruling-impact-impact-2026-11-17.md), [the record of the branches, Local impact](https://github.com/SamuelEnrique/erw/blob/main/docs/reviews/ruling-alternatives-impact-2026-11-17.md)
- **Evidence (looked up on 19 November 2026):**
  - **Published practice:** Washington Department of Revenue, "2026 Personal and Industrial Property Valuation Guidelines" (https://dor.wa.gov/sites/default/files/2025-12/2026ValuationGuidelines.docx, read today in full; the Department is the one publisher of these tables). Its alphabetical index lists six classes under "Electrical Generating" and again under "Power & Generation M&E": portable gas and diesel sets (column 16), solar panels under 1 MW (8.5), "Steam & Gas fired (fixed equipment)" (7.5), and utility-scale wind, solar and battery storage over 1 MW (RG, RG, RS). The word geothermal is nowhere in the document. For an asset the index does not list, the section "Determining the Appropriate Combined Trend Table" gives a Decision Flow Chart, printed as a picture and read today from the file: take the trend of the industry the asset typically belongs to; if that industry is not listed, the trend of a similar industry that uses the same equipment; only where there is none, ask the Department's Property Tax Division. The section "Minimum Value Percent Good Factors" sets a floor of 15 percent for assets in use, so no class is carried at full cost. The Department therefore does not name geothermal, but its own rule for an unlisted asset sends it to the trend of its industry, and column 7.5 is the only fixed thermal generating class that industry lists. That a geothermal plant's steam turbines are "the same equipment" as a steam plant's is a reading, not the Department's words.
  - **Samuel's own rulings on a like question:** None found. Searched `docs/state/rulings_2026-10-11.md` (a list of open questions on the battery, generator and datacenter pages, the machines and the licenses, which by its own words makes no ruling), `docs/state/rulings_2026-11-06.md` (five rulings, none on a tax or a schedule), the section "Rulings" of `docs/roadmap.md` (12 October 2026) and the page standard (rule 9 of `queue/done/302-day-16-november-oldlaptop-large-load-on-the-site.md`) for depreciation, schedule, at cost and upper bound. The nearest lines are the roadmap's of 12 October 2026, which took a publisher's own figure (Lazard's cost of equity, Lazard's debt share) in place of a default the tool had with no source; they are about another tool.
  - **The tool's own method note:** `docs/methods/forecast.md`, section 4 ("Every state and DC"): where no depreciation schedule was read, the value is held at the capital cost, an upper bound, said in the state's hover (marked there as a ruling). Section 10 lists Washington's geothermal plants among these because "no column names them". The rule's hover in `site/data/forecast/property_rules.json` (`wa_geothermal_property`) says the index sends unlisted assets to a flow chart printed as an image, and that column 7.5 is not the Department's assignment: the at cost rule stood on a flow chart that had not been read.
- **Recommendation after the evidence:** **switch**. The Department of Revenue's own Decision Flow Chart, read today, sends an asset its index does not list to the trend of its industry or of a similar industry using the same equipment, which for a fixed steam plant is column 7.5, and nothing in the guidelines carries any asset at full cost.

`Ruling:`

### 18. Should your family get an EV? (the game), California: California: charge at the state's average price or an off-peak plan rate

- **Page:** Should your family get an EV? (the game), California, https://erw-flame.vercel.app/play/ev
- **Question:** The game charges the electric car at the state's average home electricity price. A family that charges at night on a time-of-use plan pays that plan's off-peak rate. Which should be the default for California?
- **Now:** The state's average. On the price file main holds since this morning (August 2026's average, 29.75 cents a kWh): saved a year USD 1,707, payback 2.9 years, net present value USD 6,030.
- **Other choice:** Pacific Gas and Electric's off-peak rate on its time-of-use plan, 36.757 cents in winter (the branch's choice; eight months of the year): saved a year USD 1,381, payback 3.6 years, net present value USD 3,924. At the summer off-peak rate, 39.940 cents: USD 1,233, 4.1 years, USD 2,968. Only California has such a rate on the site; other states do not change.
- **Change:** At the winter rate: saved a year -19.1 percent, net present value -34.9 percent, payback 2.9 to 3.6 years.
- **Branch:** [wip/alt-ev-game-1](https://github.com/SamuelEnrique/erw/tree/wip/alt-ev-game-1)
- **Screenshots:** screenshot not taken yet
- **Recommendation:** **no basis to recommend**. No recommendation: the measurement and the build give this figure differently (see the foot). To weigh: the off-peak rate of that plan is above the state's average price, so the other choice makes the electric car look worse; the plan is one utility's, applied to the whole state.
- **Figures from:** [the measurement, EV game](https://github.com/SamuelEnrique/erw/blob/main/docs/reviews/ruling-impact-ev-game-2026-11-17.md), [the record of the branch, EV game](https://github.com/SamuelEnrique/erw/blob/main/docs/reviews/ruling-alternatives-ev-game-2026-11-17.md)
- **Evidence (looked up on 19 November 2026):**
  - **Published practice:** One publisher read; it uses an average by state, not a plan's rate. US Department of Energy, Alternative Fuels Data Center, Vehicle Cost Calculator, "Assumptions and Methodology" (https://afdc.energy.gov/calc/cost_calculator_methodology.html, the table of default inputs and the worked example): fuel prices are averages that a user may replace with local ones, and the electricity price of its example is a "regional average based on state"; the page names no time-of-use or off-peak rate. fueleconomy.gov (the Environmental Protection Agency's and the Department's fuel cost figures) refused the connection at its robots file, as on 16 November: not read today, and it decides nothing here. A utility's tariff was not read again today: Pacific Gas and Electric's off-peak rates stand as the site holds them (`site/data/bill_rules.json`, read 30 September 2026), one utility's plan among California's.
  - **Samuel's own rulings on a like question:** A ruling on this very default. His ruling of 14 October 2026 on this game, as his chain prompt of 6 November 2026 records it (`queue/done/198-day-6-november-portable-evgame.md`, second paragraph; `docs/methods/ev_game.md` calls it ruling 17): the electricity price is taken "from the EIA retail series by state" held in the warehouse, and every default is a figure traced to its table and row. Nothing in `docs/state/rulings_2026-10-11.md`, `docs/state/rulings_2026-11-06.md` or the roadmap's "Rulings" changes it (searched for EV, electricity price and time-of-use).
  - **The tool's own method note:** `docs/methods/ev_game.md`, "Every input, its default and its source": the electricity price is the state's newest month of `eia_retail_electricity_prices`. "The choices that move a figure" (key `home-price`): the state's residential average, every utility and hour, not an EV rate. "What the game does not hold": no time-of-use or EV rate of any utility. Its cross-check sets California's average beside Pacific Gas and Electric's off-peak rates and says why they differ: the average spreads every utility, hour and baseline credit, and that utility is the dearest of the three large ones, its rates before a baseline credit of 8.14 cents.
- **Recommendation after the evidence:** **keep**. Samuel's ruling of 14 October 2026 decided it: the game's electricity price is EIA's retail series by state, and the Department of Energy's own calculator also takes an average by state; the off-peak rate is one utility's, stands above the state's average, and would be applied to the whole state.

No second publisher was read today: no grid operator's own queue report was looked up for a withdrawn share.
  - **Samuel's own rulings on a like question:** None found. Searched `docs/state/rulings_2026-10-11.md`, `docs/state/rulings_2026-11-06.md`, the "Rulings" of `docs/roadmap.md` and the page standard for share, megawatts, withdrawn and queue.
  - **The tool's own method note:** The two notes of the queue pages differ. `docs/methods/interconnection_explorer.md`, "What it draws", item 1, and its decisions list: "The share is by count in both counts" (withdrawn over entered, by count), with no reason given. `docs/methods/interconnection_queue_summary.md`, "The rows" (the table behind `/queues`): the shares withdrawn, operating and open are written twice, of the requests and of the megawatts (`past_withdrawn_share_pct` and `past_mw_withdrawn_share_pct`), each over its own total.
- **Recommendation after the evidence:** **switch**. Berkeley Lab's practice decided it: where it counts in megawatts it gives the share withdrawn by megawatts, beside and apart from the share by requests, and the site's own `/queues` table already holds both shares; the explorer's note records by-count as its present default and gives no reason for it.

`Ruling:`

### 21. What a datacenter pays, "Your load": The headline with delivery added, once the wires utility is known

- **Page:** What a datacenter pays, "Your load", https://erw-flame.vercel.app/cost-of-power?grid=ercot&mw=300
- **Question:** The first headline number is the cost of market energy alone; the charges for delivery over the wires are in a table further down. Should the headline give energy and delivery together once the site's wires utility is known?
- **Now:** Market energy alone. A 300 MW load in Texas's North zone, the last twelve months: USD 34.07 per MWh, USD 89.54 million a year; power per GPU-hour USD 0.0691.
- **Other choice:** With the wires utility named (Oncor in the example): "energy and delivery" USD 42.87 per MWh, USD 112.67 million a year; power per GPU-hour USD 0.0869. Oncor's delivery for a flat 300 MW load is USD 8.80 per MWh (CenterPoint 7.66, AEP Texas 8.38, Texas-New Mexico Power 7.07). The summary sentence stays on market energy.
- **Change:** +8.80 USD per MWh, +25.8 percent on the headline.
- **Branch:** [wip/alt-cost-of-power-2](https://github.com/SamuelEnrique/erw/tree/wip/alt-cost-of-power-2). The utility is named in the page's address by the reader: no Texas zone has one wires utility alone (the zones hold 1 to 5).
- **Screenshots:** the other choice: [cost-of-power-2-energy-and-delivery-1280.png](https://github.com/SamuelEnrique/erw/blob/main/docs/reviews/ruling-alternatives/cost-of-power-2-energy-and-delivery-1280.png); the page today: screenshot not taken yet
- **Recommendation:** **no basis to recommend**. No allowed ground decides what the headline counts. To weigh: a reader comparing sites pays energy and delivery, about a quarter more than the headline shows; but the utility is known only when the reader names it, so the headline would mean two things depending on the address.
- **Figures from:** [the measurement, datacenter cost](https://github.com/SamuelEnrique/erw/blob/main/docs/reviews/ruling-impact-cost-of-power-2026-11-17.md), [the record of the branches, datacenter cost](https://github.com/SamuelEnrique/erw/blob/main/docs/reviews/ruling-alternatives-cost-of-power-2026-11-17.md)
- **Evidence (looked up on 19 November 2026):**
  - **Published practice:** The publishers give both figures, each under its own name, and none read adds a utility's delivery to a market price in one headline. EIA, "Electricity explained: Prices and factors affecting prices" (https://www.eia.gov/energyexplained/electricity/prices-and-factors-affecting-prices.php, sections "Many factors influence electricity prices" and "Electricity prices vary by type of customer"): the wholesale price and what a customer pays are two things; the average retail price of a sector (industrial 8.62 cents per kWh in 2025) covers the plants and the grid together. EIA, "Wholesale Electricity and Natural Gas Market Data" (https://www.eia.gov/electricity/wholesale/): hub prices of energy alone. The Independent Market Monitor for ERCOT (Potomac Economics), "2021 State of the Market Report for the ERCOT Electricity Markets" (https://www.potomaceconomics.com/wp-content/uploads/2022/05/2021-State-of-the-Market-Report.pdf), Table 1, PDF page 44 of 211: a zone's yearly price is energy alone, and transmission cost is treated apart (PDF page 68). The Public Utility Commission of Texas's pages were not read (its site answered HTTP 402).
  - **Samuel's own rulings on a like question:** His ruling 1a of 18 October 2026, recorded in `docs/methods/datacenter_pays_algorithm.md` ("Session 298: the owner's rulings of 18 October 2026", and line 14 of the page's own list of decisions): the Texas delivery table stays as built, a table of its own with one row a component, never added to the market cost. Also the roadmap's rulings of 12 October 2026 (`docs/roadmap.md`): scenario A drives the headline, and every assumption shows its default and is editable in place; "Delivery and other charges" is such an assumption of scenario A, so a reader can already add a utility's total there.
  - **The tool's own method note:** `docs/methods/datacenter_pays_algorithm.md`, section 12, line 1: the cost is wholesale energy alone at a hub or load zone, no delivery, transmission, capacity, ancillary or retail charge is added, and a reader adds one as a scenario's "Delivery and other charges". Line 14 repeats that Texas's delivery charges are never added to the market cost. The record of the branch adds that in no Texas zone is the wires utility one of the four tariff utilities alone (the zones hold 1 to 5 wires utilities), so the utility is known only when the reader names it in the address.
- **Recommendation after the evidence:** **keep**. Samuel's ruling 1a of 18 October 2026 decided it: Texas's delivery charges stand in their own table and are never added to the market cost, which the method note's first line also states as the page's rule.

`Ruling:`
