### 23. Demand growth, California: California: the 2019 base weeks from the grid operator's own load

- **Page:** Demand growth, California, https://erw-flame.vercel.app/demand?grid=caiso
- **Question:** California's growth since 2019 starts from EIA's figures for late February and March 2019, which run about 3,000 MW above the grid operator's own load in those weeks. Should the base weeks be read from the grid operator's own load?
- **Now:** EIA's file: California's change in average demand from 2019 to 2025 is 1,089.3 MW.
- **Other choice:** The grid operator's own load in the base weeks: 847.5 MW.
- **Change:** -241.8 MW, -22.2 percent; every growth figure of California moves with it.
- **Branch:** not built: the grid operator's hourly load for February and March 2019 is not on the old laptop (its copy starts in September 2021); the figures are the data machine's, from its note of 17 November.
- **Screenshots:** screenshot not taken yet
- **Recommendation:** **no basis to recommend**. No allowed ground decides which source the base weeks stand on, and the figures could not be recomputed where the list was written. To weigh: the rest of California's series is EIA's, so the other choice mixes two sources in one growth figure; keeping EIA's base keeps a known overstatement of about 3,000 MW in five weeks of 2019.
- **Figures from:** [the measurement, demand growth](https://github.com/SamuelEnrique/erw/blob/main/docs/reviews/ruling-impact-demand-2026-11-17.md), [the record of the branches, demand growth](https://github.com/SamuelEnrique/erw/blob/main/docs/reviews/ruling-alternatives-demand-2026-11-17.md)
- **Evidence (looked up on 19 November 2026):**
  - **Published practice:** The publishers do not make this choice: neither EIA nor CAISO publishes a growth figure whose base weeks are spliced from the other's series. What was read bears on whether the two series are one measure. CAISO, Today's Outlook (https://www.caiso.com/todays-outlook, the notes under the demand charts): its demand figures "do not include dispatchable pump loads and battery storage that is charging", so CAISO's own load and EIA's demand for California are not defined alike. EIA, Today in Energy, "EIA launches redesigned Hourly Electric Grid Monitor with new data and functionality" (21 August 2019, https://www.eia.gov/todayinenergy/detail.php?id=40993): the figures are what the balancing authorities report on Form EIA-930. How EIA treats a reported hour it finds faulty is on the monitor's "About" page, which gave no text to the fetch (drawn by script), and in the form's instructions, whose path EIA's robots file disallows: not read today, so it decides nothing.
  - **Samuel's own rulings on a like question:** Two, both on reading California from CAISO's own data in place of EIA's. (1) The ruling on the break in EIA's California series, live from 4 October 2026 (`docs/methods/eia930_caiso_break.md`, "The join (the ruling)"): CAISO's own supply replaces EIA's for generation from 16 December 2025, and EIA-930 "stays the source" for demand on both sides of that date; a period is written from one source or not at all, never mixed. (2) The Grid ruling of 18 October 2026 on the gravity shape (`docs/methods/grid_network.md`, "One page, four tabs, and the Ask panel"): CAISO's own figure is not substituted for EIA's, because the shape would then mix two sources; the difference is said on the page. Nothing on the 2019 base weeks in `docs/state/rulings_2026-10-11.md`, `docs/state/rulings_2026-11-06.md` or the roadmap's "Rulings" (searched: California, CAISO, 2019, base).
  - **The tool's own method note:** `docs/methods/demand_growth.md`, the paragraph "California's 2019, the base" (session 7022): EIA's demand stands about 3,000 MW under CAISO's own load from 26 February to 31 March 2019 (EIA's mean 19,547.7 MW, CAISO's 22,438.6); the hours pass the screen, so the table holds them as published, the register of source faults names them, and reading them from CAISO's load is left to the owner. The same note's rule on hours: an hour not used is used for nothing, and nothing is filled. The other choice would write 2019's mean from 331 days of EIA and 34 days of CAISO's load times 0.9865.
- **Recommendation after the evidence:** **keep**. Samuel's ruling on this same series, live from 4 October 2026, decides it: California's demand stays EIA-930's and no period is written from two sources, and his ruling of 18 October on the gravity shape refused the same substitution, so the known gap stays said on the page and in the faults register.

`Ruling:`

### 24. Grid, the Mix tab: The Mix tab's demand from the hourly snapshot

- **Page:** Grid, the Mix tab, https://erw-flame.vercel.app/grid?grid=ercot&view=mix
- **Question:** Should the Mix tab's demand "right now" be read from the hourly snapshot the Network tab uses, instead of the daily table, which is about a day behind it?
- **Now:** The daily table's newest hour. Read on 17 November it was 15 November 23:00 UTC: ERCOT 54,008 MW, PJM 98,443 MW, SPP 33,770 MW, CAISO 23,294 MW.
- **Other choice:** The hourly snapshot's demand where its hour is newer (17 November 06:00 UTC, 31 hours newer): ERCOT 46,760 MW, PJM 90,018 MW, SPP 30,799 MW, CAISO 23,425 MW. The comparison with the same hour a week before would still be read from the daily table.
- **Change:** ERCOT -13.42 percent, PJM -8.56 percent, SPP -8.80 percent, CAISO +0.56 percent; NYISO -20.25 percent and ISO-NE -18.86 percent (their figures stay held on the page). Most of each change is the hour of the day, late afternoon against midnight Central, not a change in the grid.
- **Branch:** [wip/alt-grid-2](https://github.com/SamuelEnrique/erw/tree/wip/alt-grid-2)
- **Screenshots:** screenshot not taken yet
- **Recommendation:** **no basis to recommend**. Nothing in the allowed grounds decides which of the site's two demand sources the tab reads. To weigh: the other choice makes the Mix tab and the Network tab show the same hour; the national figure (the Lower 48) is not in the snapshot and would stay on the daily table; the week-before comparison would mix the two sources.
- **Figures from:** [the measurement, Grid](https://github.com/SamuelEnrique/erw/blob/main/docs/reviews/ruling-impact-grid-2026-11-17.md), [the record of the branches, Grid](https://github.com/SamuelEnrique/erw/blob/main/docs/reviews/ruling-alternatives-grid-2026-11-17.md)
- **Evidence (looked up on 19 November 2026):**
  - **Published practice:** Two publishers, and they agree: demand "now" is the newest reading the publisher has. EIA, Today in Energy, "EIA's Hourly Electric Grid Monitor provides timely data about electricity usage patterns" (6 April 2020, https://www.eia.gov/todayinenergy/detail.php?id=43295) and "Product Highlight: EIA's Hourly Electric Grid Monitor" (6 December 2022, https://www.eia.gov/todayinenergy/detail.php?id=54899): the monitor "is updated every hour" with the demand of the hour before, while generation and total interchange lag a day. CAISO, Today's Outlook (https://www.caiso.com/todays-outlook): the lead figure is current demand, the power needed on the grid at that moment, with its trend on 5-minute averages. Neither shows a demand a day old where it holds a newer one.
  - **Samuel's own rulings on a like question:** None found on which of the site's two copies of EIA's demand a tab reads (searched `docs/state/rulings_2026-10-11.md`, `docs/state/rulings_2026-11-06.md`, the roadmap's "Rulings" and the page standard for: demand, snapshot, hourly, Mix, now). Near it: the Grid ruling of 18 October 2026 on the age of "now" (`docs/methods/grid_network.md`, "One page, four tabs, and the Ask panel") has the page say how old its newest hour is. His ruling of the same day against mixing two sources on the gravity shape does not bear: both copies are EIA-930's demand from the same route of EIA's service (`docs/methods/eia930_all_demand.md`; `docs/methods/grid_network.md`, "The hourly run"), and the record of the branch found them equal at the hours both hold.
  - **The tool's own method note:** `docs/methods/grid_network.md`, "How far behind the newest hour is, and why" (session 247): "Demand is not behind": EIA publishes it one to two hours after the hour, and the page shows each grid's demand at its own newest hour; "The hourly run" pulls the seven operators' demand every hour, with a check key of its own. `docs/methods/eia930_all_demand.md`: the daily table holds whole UTC days only, so its newest hour is 23:00 UTC of a day at least a day back. The tab's own sentence (`site/components/grid/NowView.tsx`) calls its demand hour "the latest EIA has published", which holds for the snapshot's hour and not for the daily table's.
- **Recommendation after the evidence:** **switch**. The method note decides it: the Grid page's rule is demand at the newest hour EIA has published, the Mix tab's own words claim that hour, and only the hourly snapshot holds it (EIA's and CAISO's practice agree); the Lower 48, which the snapshot does not hold, and the week-before figure stay on the daily table, as the branch built it.

`Ruling:`

### 25. Shoulder hours, December 2025: Whose battery fleet: EIA's or the market monitors'

- **Page:** Shoulder hours, December 2025, https://erw-flame.vercel.app/shoulder?grid=ercot&month=2025-12
- **Question:** The battery fleet the shoulder is set against is EIA's count of batteries. The market monitors report larger fleets at the end of 2025. Whose count should the page use?
- **Now:** EIA's monthly generator inventory. Texas, December 2025: 13,909 MW and 21,579 MWh; 2.43 hours needed; the fleet covers 1.55 hours. California by the grid operator's own data: 15,760 MW; 2.87 hours needed.
- **Other choice:** The monitors' year-end counts, for December 2025 only (they publish no monthly series). Texas: "more than 17,000 MW" and 31,500 MWh (Potomac Economics, 2025 State of the Market Report, June 2026, page ii), taken as 17,000 MW: 1.99 hours needed, the fleet covers 1.85 hours. California: "over 17 GW" (the grid operator's Department of Market Monitoring, 2025 annual report, page 23): 2.66 hours needed; the hours the fleet covers cannot be given, since no MWh is published. Every other month stays EIA's.
- **Change:** Texas: hours needed -0.44, -18.2 percent; hours the fleet covers +0.30, +19.4 percent. California: hours needed -7.3 percent.
- **Branch:** [wip/alt-shoulder-5](https://github.com/SamuelEnrique/erw/tree/wip/alt-shoulder-5)
- **Screenshots:** screenshot not taken yet
- **Recommendation:** **no basis to recommend**. Both counts are published and cited; no ruling or method rule says which is the fleet. To weigh: the monitors' figures are bounds ("more than", "over"), not one number, and exist for one month, so December 2025 would stand on another source than every other month and California's December would be incomplete.
- **Figures from:** [the measurement, shoulder hours](https://github.com/SamuelEnrique/erw/blob/main/docs/reviews/ruling-impact-shoulder-2026-11-17.md), [the record of the branches, shoulder hours](https://github.com/SamuelEnrique/erw/blob/main/docs/reviews/ruling-alternatives-shoulder-2026-11-17.md)
- **Evidence (looked up on 19 November 2026):**
  - **Published practice:** The publishers make it both ways: each counts the fleet from its own source, and none calls the other's count wrong. Potomac Economics, *2025 State of the Market Report for the ERCOT Electricity Markets* (June 2026, https://www.potomaceconomics.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/06/2025-State-of-the-Market-Report-for-ERCOT.pdf, page ii): the storage resources registered with ERCOT reached "more than 17,000 MW" and 31,500 MWh by the end of 2025; pages 13 and 21 give their average duration as 1.83 hours, which with 31,500 MWh is about 17,200 MW, so 17,000 is a floor and not the count. CAISO's Department of Market Monitoring, *2025 Annual Report on Market Issues and Performance* (June 2026, https://www.caiso.com/documents/2025-annual-report-on-market-issues-and-performance.pdf, page 23): over 17 GW in the CAISO area at the end of 2025, with no MWh. The same department's *2024 Special Report on Battery Storage* (29 May 2025, https://www.caiso.com/documents/2024-special-report-on-battery-storage-may-29-2025.pdf, pages 6 and 17, PDF pages 7 and 18) counts from the operator's own registry, sets EIA's count beside it in a chart, and puts the gap down to "differences in on-line dates", MW ratings and how hybrid plants are rated. EIA, Today in Energy, "Solar generation in CAISO surpassed natural gas in the first five months of 2026" (16 June 2026, https://www.eia.gov/todayinenergy/detail.php?id=67784): 16 GW in California, from its own Preliminary Monthly Electric Generator Inventory, the source the page uses. The monitors print one year-end figure each, no monthly series.
  - **Samuel's own rulings on a like question:** None found on which fleet (searched `docs/state/rulings_2026-10-11.md`, `docs/state/rulings_2026-11-06.md` and the roadmap's "Rulings" for fleet, inventory and monitor). Two of his standing rules bear on the other choice. `CLAUDE.md`, non-negotiable 1: real data only, never a filled-in number; the other choice takes a published floor ("more than") as 17,000 MW. The page standard (rule 9 of `queue/done/302-day-16-november-oldlaptop-large-load-on-the-site.md`, 16 November 2026): an upgrade drops nothing the page had; under the other choice California's December 2025 loses the hours its fleet covers (3.44 today), because the monitor prints no MWh.
  - **The tool's own method note:** `docs/methods/shoulder_hours.md`, "Inputs": the fleet is `storage_buildout_monthly`, EIA's operating batteries of the grid and month. `docs/methods/shoulder.md`, section 9, already sets the page's figures beside both monitors' and gives the reason for the gap (owners' reports to EIA against the operator's registry); section 10, item 1: default taken, EIA's inventory stays, one source for both grids and monthly since 2015, and the question waits for the owner.
- **Recommendation after the evidence:** **keep**. Samuel's standing rules decided it: the monitors print a floor for one month and, for California, no MWh, so the other choice would stand December 2025 on a number no publisher printed and blank a California figure the page has; the publishers themselves each keep to their own count, EIA's being the only monthly one.

`Ruling:`

The Southwest Power Pool: September +17.28. New York: July -10.49. The Lower 48: August +12.16. November and December stay at 2025.
- **Change:** Texas September +17.08 points, March +11.55, February -5.73; California October +13.34 and July turns from a fall to a rise; 80 cells (8 grids by 10 months) change.
- **Branch:** [wip/alt-demand-growth-2](https://github.com/SamuelEnrique/erw/tree/wip/alt-demand-growth-2)

- **Figures from:** [the measurement, demand growth explorer](https://github.com/SamuelEnrique/erw/blob/main/docs/reviews/ruling-impact-demand-growth-2026-11-17.md), [the record of the branches, demand growth explorer](https://github.com/SamuelEnrique/erw/blob/main/docs/reviews/ruling-alternatives-demand-growth-2026-11-17.md)
- **Evidence (looked up on 19 November 2026):**
  - **Published practice:** Two publishers, and they agree: the newest month is set against the same month of an earlier year as soon as it is published, without waiting for the year to end. EIA, Electric Power Monthly, Table ES1.A, "Total Electric Power Industry Summary Statistics, 2026 and 2025" (https://www.eia.gov/electricity/monthly/epm_table_grapher.php?t=table_es1a): August 2026 beside August 2025 with a "Percentage Change" column; its Table 5.1 (https://www.eia.gov/electricity/monthly/epm_table_grapher.php?t=epmt_5_01) gives the months of 2026 to August and the year to date over the same months of 2024, 2025 and 2026. ERCOT, Monthly Operational Overview (August 2026), 16 September 2026 (https://www.ercot.com/files/docs/2026/09/16/ERCOT-Monthly-Operational-Overview-August-2026.pdf), pages 2 and 3: the month's peak against "the August 2025 peak demand", and the month's energy up 10.1 percent on August 2025.
  - **Samuel's own rulings on a like question:** None found on which year a month's view reads (searched `docs/state/rulings_2026-10-11.md`, `docs/state/rulings_2026-11-06.md`, the roadmap's "Rulings" and the page standard for: month, whole year, newest, year so far). Near it, and not against the other choice: the roadmap ruling of 12 October 2026 that the battery headline counts whole months only; the other choice reads whole months only (January to October 2026).
  - **The tool's own method note:** `docs/methods/demand_growth.md`, the explorer's decisions (`last-whole-year`): a month's growth is read at the last whole year, because the table writes a month's growth on that year's row only. The same note lists exactly this first under "For the owner's ruling" (a month the newest year already holds shows its megawatts and no growth; default: left as it is), so the note states today's rule and leaves the choice open. Its rule for the year so far (decision 4 of `/demand`) already sets the newest, partial year against the same months of 2019.
- **Recommendation after the evidence:** **switch**. Published practice decides it: EIA's Electric Power Monthly and ERCOT's monthly overview both set the newest month against the same month of an earlier year while the year is still running, which is also how the tool's own year-so-far figure already compares.

`Ruling:`

### 27. What a battery saves a customer: Peak shaving at 85 percent of the battery's power

- **Page:** What a battery saves a customer, https://erw-flame.vercel.app/battery/customer
- **Question:** "From shaving the peak" takes the battery's whole power off every month's peak. A national laboratory found about 85 percent a more reasonable expectation. Should the page apply 85 percent?
- **Now:** The whole power, an upper bound. Austin Energy: "Saved a year" USD 8,931.16. Southern California Edison's 2013 rate: USD 3,014.00 a month from the peak, USD 44,123.70 a year. Seattle City Light: USD 12,795.06.
- **Other choice:** 85 percent of it (National Renewable Energy Laboratory, report TP-5400-63162, January 2015, page 16). Austin USD 7,549.66. Edison USD 2,561.90 a month, USD 38,698.50 a year. Seattle USD 11,830.26. The page names the report.
- **Change:** "From shaving the peak" -15 percent in every case; "Saved a year" -15.5 percent (Austin), -12.3 percent (Edison), -7.5 percent (Seattle).
- **Branch:** [wip/alt-battery-customer-3](https://github.com/SamuelEnrique/erw/tree/wip/alt-battery-customer-3)
- **Screenshots:** screenshot not taken yet
- **Recommendation:** **switch**. A published figure checks it: the laboratory's report, on 98 buildings' measured loads, says "85% of the rated system power is a more reasonable expectation when the rated power duration is 4 hours", against the page's 100 percent. The limit to know: the figure is for a 4-hour battery, and the report gives less for shorter ones (about 60 percent at 30 minutes).
- **Figures from:** [the measurement, customer battery](https://github.com/SamuelEnrique/erw/blob/main/docs/reviews/ruling-impact-battery-customer-2026-11-17.md), [the record of the branches, customer battery](https://github.com/SamuelEnrique/erw/blob/main/docs/reviews/ruling-alternatives-battery-customer-2026-11-17.md)

### 28. Where power contracts are being struck: Leave market operators out of "rows signed"

- **Page:** Where power contracts are being struck, https://erw-flame.vercel.app/contracts
- **Question:** The headline count of contract rows signed includes rows whose buyer is a market operator (PJM, MISO and the like), which are market sales, not contracts between two companies. Should those rows be left out?
- **Now:** Counted. The opening view (the second quarter of 2026; energy, capacity and tolling): 227 rows signed, energy 126, capacity 100. The fourth quarter of 2025: 311.
- **Other choice:** Left out: 192 rows; energy 100, capacity 91. The fourth quarter of 2025: 263. Over the 12 quarters listed, 3,778 rows become 3,422. The operators matched by name: PJM (181 of the 356 rows), MISO, ISO New England, California's, the Southwest Power Pool, New York's.
- **Change:** The opening headline -35 rows, -15.4 percent; energy -20.6 percent, capacity -9.0 percent; all 12 quarters -9.4 percent.
- **Branch:** [wip/alt-contracts-3](https://github.com/SamuelEnrique/erw/tree/wip/alt-contracts-3)
- **Screenshots:** screenshot not taken yet
- **Recommendation:** **no basis to recommend**. No allowed ground says what counts as a contract signed. To weigh: a sale to a market operator is a settlement, not a deal between a buyer and a seller; the other choice needs a ruled list of operator names (the measurement's list of seven is a default, matched by the words of the buyer's name).
- **Figures from:** [the measurement, contracts](https://github.com/SamuelEnrique/erw/blob/main/docs/reviews/ruling-impact-contracts-2026-11-17.md), [the record of the branches, contracts](https://github.com/SamuelEnrique/erw/blob/main/docs/reviews/ruling-alternatives-contracts-2026-11-17.md)
- **Evidence (looked up on 19 November 2026):**
  - **Published practice:** FERC, Electric Quarterly Report Data Dictionary, Version 3.5, issued 23 November 2020 (https://www.ferc.gov/sites/default/files/2020-11/Data_Dictionary_V3_5_Clean.pdf; whether a later version is in force was not read today). Contract Data, Field 17 (page 4): the customer is "the purchaser of contract products and services", whoever that is; the form has no rule that leaves a market operator out of the contract file. Transaction Data, Field 55 (page 12): a sale made to a regional operator is reported like any other and marked by its own type of rate, "RTO/ISO". So FERC keeps a sale to a market operator in the report and marks it; it does not drop it. FERC publishes no count of contracts signed, so it does not make the headline's choice itself. No second publisher of these filings was read.
  - **Samuel's own rulings on a like question:** The page standard, rule 9 of the chain file of 16 November 2026 (`queue/done/302-day-16-november-oldlaptop-large-load-on-the-site.md`): an upgrade "drops nothing the page had". The other choice takes 35 rows out of the opening list and out of every count of the first view. No ruling names market operators or says what counts as a contract signed (searched the two rulings files and the roadmap's rulings for operator, contract, counterparty, settlement and rows).
  - **The tool's own method note:** `docs/methods/contracts.md`, section 3: the headline's rows are the count of the rows of the quarter, product and authority chosen, and every cell of the list is as filed; decision 1: the page reads what was agreed in the contract file, not what was delivered or billed. The note has no rule that sets a buyer aside by who it is, and no list of operator names.
- **Recommendation after the evidence:** **keep**. FERC's own definition decided it: the customer of a contract row is whoever purchases, and a sale to a regional operator is kept in the report and marked, not left out; Samuel's page standard (nothing the page had is dropped) and the note's as-filed list agree. A count of the operator rows beside the headline, as FERC marks them, would be a third choice that neither branch builds.

`Ruling:`

### 29. Where power contracts are being struck: Is a rate filed as 0 a price

- **Page:** Where power contracts are being struck, https://erw-flame.vercel.app/contracts
- **Question:** The headline "Rows with a price as a number" counts a rate filed as 0 as a price. Is a 0 a price?
- **Now:** A 0 is counted. The opening view: 112 of 227 rows with a price; energy 56, capacity 56. The fourth quarter of 2025: 104 of 311. The terms view's count over the quarter's whole file: 81,718 rows.
- **Other choice:** A 0 is not counted: 95 of 227; energy 45, capacity 50. The fourth quarter of 2025: 65. The terms view: 44,643, with the zeros counted on their own.
- **Change:** The opening headline -17 rows, -15.2 percent; the fourth quarter of 2025 -37.5 percent; the terms view -45.4 percent.
- **Branch:** [wip/alt-contracts-2](https://github.com/SamuelEnrique/erw/tree/wip/alt-contracts-2)
- **Screenshots:** screenshot not taken yet
- **Recommendation:** **no basis to recommend**. No allowed ground says whether a filed 0 is a price. To weigh: 301 of the 1,314 numbers listed over 12 quarters are 0; a seller rarely sells power for nothing, so most zeros likely mean no fixed rate was filed, but the filings do not say which.
- **Figures from:** [the measurement, contracts](https://github.com/SamuelEnrique/erw/blob/main/docs/reviews/ruling-impact-contracts-2026-11-17.md), [the record of the branches, contracts](https://github.com/SamuelEnrique/erw/blob/main/docs/reviews/ruling-alternatives-contracts-2026-11-17.md)
- **Evidence (looked up on 19 November 2026):**
  - **Published practice:** FERC, Electric Quarterly Report Data Dictionary, Version 3.5, issued 23 November 2020 (https://www.ferc.gov/sites/default/files/2020-11/Data_Dictionary_V3_5_Clean.pdf, Contract Data, Fields 34 to 37, pages 8 and 9; whether a later version is in force was not read today). The Rate field is "the charge for the product per unit as stated in the contract", a number; where the contract states no rate the field is to stay blank, and the filer gives one of the other three rate fields instead (a minimum, a maximum, or the rate in words). The form says nowhere that a 0 means "no rate": where it wants a zero for "nothing to report" it says so (Field 69, the transmission charge, page 14). So by FERC's own rule a 0 in the Rate field is a number the filer filed as the contract's charge, and a contract with no fixed rate should carry a blank. FERC does not say how often filers break that rule. No second publisher of these filings was read.
  - **Samuel's own rulings on a like question:** None found. Searched `docs/state/rulings_2026-10-11.md`, `docs/state/rulings_2026-11-06.md`, the "Rulings" of `docs/roadmap.md` and the page standard for zero, price, rate and number. The page standard's "real numbers only, never a placeholder" does not settle it: the 0 is the filer's, not the ERW's.
  - **The tool's own method note:** `docs/methods/contracts.md`, section 12 ("The priced rows whose number is 0"), states the rule and its reason: the note under the headline says how many of the rows counted file the number 0, and they stay counted among the rows with a price as a number, because the filer filed a number. Against that, the same tool reads megawatts the other way: `docs/methods/eqr_terms.md`, "Megawatts, where a quantity is stated": a quantity of zero is not a quantity.
- **Recommendation after the evidence:** **keep**. FERC's data dictionary decided it: a number in the Rate field is the charge as the contract states it and a contract with no rate is to leave the field blank, so the headline "rows with a price as a number" is true of a filed 0, and the method note already has the page say how many of them are 0.

`Ruling:`

- **Question:** San Diego Gas and Electric gives a credit on use up to a baseline allowance that depends on the home's climate zone. The page's default San Diego bill has no allowance, so no credit. Should the page read the allowances by zone?
- **Now:** A 0 kWh allowance: the default bill is USD 332.91 for 600 kWh, 55.48 cents a kWh. EIA's 2024 average for the utility's homes is 43.63 cents.
- **Other choice:** A zone's allowance. The credit is USD 0.10702 a kWh on use up to 130 percent of the allowance: USD 13.91 off for every 100 kWh of monthly allowance. At 100, 200 or 300 kWh (examples): USD 319.00, 305.08 or 291.17; 53.17 to 48.53 cents a kWh.
- **Change:** -4.2, -8.4 or -12.5 percent on the examples; the true figure needs the zones' allowances.
- **Branch:** not built: the zones' own allowances are not held, so the default zone's bill cannot be computed.
- **Screenshots:** screenshot not taken yet
- **Recommendation:** **switch**. A published figure checks it: EIA's 2024 average price for the utility's homes is 43.63 cents a kWh, and the page's default bill is 55.48 cents, about 27 percent above it; a baseline credit moves the bill toward the published average. It can only be done once the allowances are read from the tariff.
- **Figures from:** [the measurement, electricity bill](https://github.com/SamuelEnrique/erw/blob/main/docs/reviews/ruling-impact-bill-2026-11-17.md), [the record of the branches, electricity bill](https://github.com/SamuelEnrique/erw/blob/main/docs/reviews/ruling-alternatives-bill-2026-11-17.md)

### 31. The deal tracker, megawatts disclosed: A folded duplicate's figure carried to the deal kept

- **Page:** The deal tracker, megawatts disclosed, https://erw-flame.vercel.app/deals
- **Question:** When two news rows are the same deal, the tracker keeps one and folds the other. If only the folded row states the megawatts, the figure is lost. Should the kept row take a figure that only the folded row states, as the warehouse's own merge does?
- **Now:** The kept row as read: megawatts disclosed 40,297 MW by 35 deals.
- **Other choice:** The folded row's figure carried: 40,730 MW by 36 deals. One deal gains its megawatts: the 433 MW of the Energy Department's loan to Vistra. A figure the kept row states is never replaced.
- **Change:** +433 MW, +1.07 percent (+1.0 percent on the live site's larger table).
- **Branch:** [wip/alt-deals-2](https://github.com/SamuelEnrique/erw/tree/wip/alt-deals-2). Five older tests that hold the figures as main computes them are left failing on the branch; they would move with the ruling.
- **Screenshots:** the other choice: [deals-2-folded-figure-held-1280.png](https://github.com/SamuelEnrique/erw/blob/main/docs/reviews/ruling-alternatives/deals-2-folded-figure-held-1280.png); the page today: screenshot not taken yet
- **Recommendation:** **no basis to recommend**. No allowed ground decides it. To weigh: the warehouse's own merge already carries such a figure, so the page and the table differ; the figure carried is one the news source states, not one the ERW makes.
- **Figures from:** [the measurement, deal tracker](https://github.com/SamuelEnrique/erw/blob/main/docs/reviews/ruling-impact-deals-2026-11-17.md), [the record of the branches, deal tracker](https://github.com/SamuelEnrique/erw/blob/main/docs/reviews/ruling-alternatives-deals-2026-11-17.md)
- **Evidence (looked up on 19 November 2026):**
  - **Published practice:** Not found. The two public trackers read say what a deal must be to count and do not say what happens to a figure when two reports of one deal are joined: the Clean Energy Buyers Association's Deal Tracker (https://ceba.org/deal-tracker/, the inclusion criteria; the last quarter of that long page was not read) and the Clean Investment Monitor (https://www.cleaninvestmentmonitor.org/methodology, "How we categorize investment", which says only that a company's own reported amount is used where there is one). So published practice does not decide it.
  - **Samuel's own rulings on a like question:** The human ruling at the head of `archive/sessions/SESSION_16_PROMPT.md` (late September 2026): the extractor must not infer beyond the stated words, and empty is correct where the words state nothing. The tracker's decision 2 (`docs/methods/deal_tracker.md`, section 11) rests on it: a figure is kept only where a story's own words state it, and "not stated" is for a figure no story states. The 433 MW are stated by a story of the folded pair, so carrying them infers nothing. Nothing on folding in `docs/state/rulings_2026-10-11.md`, `docs/state/rulings_2026-11-06.md`, the roadmap's rulings or the page standard (searched for fold, duplicate, merge and carry).
  - **The tool's own method note:** The notes state the rule for a join and record the fold as the one place that departs from it. `docs/methods/energy_deals.md`, "One record a deal", and `docs/methods/deal_tracker.md`, section 3, step 2: when two rows are one deal, the join keeps the earliest date, moves the stage forward, "fills a gap and never replaces a figure held". Section 3 then says the register's folded pairs do not do this (the row kept stands as extracted; today that loses 433 MW and USD 0.2 billion of one loan), and section 12, item 5, lists it as waiting for the owner. The loan in question, the Energy Department's to Vistra, is the same pair `energy_deals.md` ("One deal across days") gives as its example of two rows that are one deal.
- **Recommendation after the evidence:** **switch**. The tool's own method note decided it: its rule for two rows of one deal is that the join fills a figure the kept row lacks and never replaces one it holds, the register's fold is that same join made later by a person, and the figure carried is one a story states, which Samuel's ruling of session 16 on stated words allows.

`Ruling:`

## Section 2: lines that change another figure

### 32. Local impact (the forecast tool), Kansas: Kansas: assume the ten-year exemption is granted

- **Page:** Local impact (the forecast tool), Kansas, https://erw-flame.vercel.app/impact
- **Question:** Kansas exempts a renewable plant from property tax for ten years, on application. The tool assumes the exemption is granted. Should it?
- **Now:** Granted. Kansas wind as it opens: years 1 to 10 read USD 0 and the life has no total. With a tax rate typed (1.5 percent over 25 years, an example): USD 22.16 million over the life, USD 7.90 million in present value.
- **Other choice:** Not assumed: as it opens years 1 to 10 read "not held". With the same rate typed: USD 60.02 million over the life, USD 35.21 million in present value.
- **Change:** With a rate typed, property tax over the life +170.8 percent; in present value USD 7.90 million to 35.21 million. As it opens, ten cells go from USD 0 to "not held".
- **Branch:** [wip/alt-impact-4](https://github.com/SamuelEnrique/erw/tree/wip/alt-impact-4)
- **Screenshots:** the other choice: [impact-4-1280.png](https://github.com/SamuelEnrique/erw/blob/main/docs/reviews/ruling-alternatives/impact-4-1280.png), [impact-4-own-1280.png](https://github.com/SamuelEnrique/erw/blob/main/docs/reviews/ruling-alternatives/impact-4-own-1280.png), [impact-4-table-1280.png](https://github.com/SamuelEnrique/erw/blob/main/docs/reviews/ruling-alternatives/impact-4-table-1280.png); the page today: [impact-4-default-1280.png](https://github.com/SamuelEnrique/erw/blob/main/docs/reviews/ruling-alternatives/impact-4-default-1280.png), [impact-4-own-default-1280.png](https://github.com/SamuelEnrique/erw/blob/main/docs/reviews/ruling-alternatives/impact-4-own-default-1280.png), [impact-4-table-default-1280.png](https://github.com/SamuelEnrique/erw/blob/main/docs/reviews/ruling-alternatives/impact-4-table-default-1280.png)
- **Recommendation:** **no basis to recommend**. No allowed ground says whether a statute's exemption that needs an application is assumed granted. To weigh: the statute gives the exemption and the tool reads statutes as defaults elsewhere; but it is granted case by case, and the tool treats other discretionary abatements (Alabama's, Nevada's) as not assumed.
- **Figures from:** [the measurement, Local impact](https://github.com/SamuelEnrique/erw/blob/main/docs/reviews/ruling-impact-impact-2026-11-17.md), [the record of the branches, Local impact](https://github.com/SamuelEnrique/erw/blob/main/docs/reviews/ruling-alternatives-impact-2026-11-17.md)
- **Evidence (looked up on 19 November 2026):**
  - **Published practice:** Two Kansas publishers, read today, agree that the exemption is the statute's and that the application is the ordinary way any Kansas exemption is claimed. (1) Kansas Office of Revisor of Statutes, K.S.A. 79-201 (https://ksrevisor.gov/statutes/chapters/ch79/079_002_0001.html): its opening line says the property it lists "shall be and is hereby exempt", and paragraph Eleventh lists all property used predominantly to generate electricity from renewable resources, for the 10 taxable years after construction where the application is filed after 31 December 2016. K.S.A. 79-213 (https://ksrevisor.gov/statutes/chapters/ch79/079_002_0013.html), subsections (a) to (g) and (l): every owner asking for any exemption files a request with the county appraiser, who recommends, and the Board of Tax Appeals decides; subsection (l) lists the 25 kinds of property that need no request, and renewable plants are not among them. So the request is the general procedure (a church or a hospital files the same one), not a grant special to power plants. (2) Kansas Department of Revenue, Division of Property Valuation, memorandum to county appraisers "Renewable Energy Resources & Battery Energy Storage Systems (BESS)", 21 November 2024 (https://ksrevenue.gov/pdf/RenEnergyExempt-BESS.pdf), pages 2 and 4: renewable exemptions "must be applied for and granted by" the Board under the process of K.S.A. 79-213; the Board determines the exemption and its period; a project granted it is exempt for ten years. The memorandum then tells appraisers how to value a plant once its ten exempt years have ended (20 percent of its retail cost when new, assessed at 25 percent, where it sells to wholesale customers only). Neither document gives a county or the Board a choice to refuse property that meets the statute's words, unlike Nevada's abatement (the Director approves, the county may deny) which the tool does not assume. The memorandum also says a doubt whether an item qualifies is resolved in favour of taxation, which is about what is in the plant (a battery), not about whether a plant is granted.
  - **Samuel's own rulings on a like question:** None found. Searched `docs/state/rulings_2026-10-11.md` (no ruling in it, and nothing on this tool), `docs/state/rulings_2026-11-06.md`, the section "Rulings" of `docs/roadmap.md` and the page standard (rule 9 of `queue/done/302-day-16-november-oldlaptop-large-load-on-the-site.md`) for exemption, application, granted, abatement and assume.
  - **The tool's own method note:** `docs/methods/forecast.md`, section 4, "exempt for a number of years": Kansas exempts property generating electricity from wind, solar or geothermal for the 10 taxable years after construction, and the tool charges 0 for those years and holds no rule after them. Section 5 gives the note's rule for the other kind: a discretionary abatement (Alabama, Nevada) is held at 0 and said why. The rule's entry in `site/data/forecast/property_rules.json` (`ks_renewable_exempt`) says the Board grants it on application and the tool assumes it is granted.
- **Recommendation after the evidence:** **keep**. The statute and the Department of Revenue's memorandum, read today, both treat the ten years as the statute's exemption claimed through the request every Kansas exemption needs, not as an abatement a body may refuse, which is the kind the method note leaves unassumed.

`Ruling:`

### 34. Local impact (the forecast tool), Wisconsin solar: Wisconsin solar: does the exemption reach electrical materials

- **Page:** Local impact (the forecast tool), Wisconsin solar, https://erw-flame.vercel.app/impact
- **Question:** Wisconsin's sales tax exemption for solar is read as covering the mounting, modules and inverters (83.6 percent of the purchases). Does it reach the electrical materials too?
- **Now:** 83.6 percent exempt. Wisconsin solar as it opens: sales and use tax USD 1.48 million.
- **Other choice:** Up to 100 percent exempt: sales and use tax as low as USD 0. The electrical materials' share of the remaining purchases is not held, so the true figure lies between USD 1.48 million and 0.
- **Change:** Between 0 and -100 percent; the branch shows the bound, -100 percent.
- **Branch:** [wip/alt-impact-5](https://github.com/SamuelEnrique/erw/tree/wip/alt-impact-5)
- **Screenshots:** the other choice: [impact-5-1280.png](https://github.com/SamuelEnrique/erw/blob/main/docs/reviews/ruling-alternatives/impact-5-1280.png), [impact-5-table-1280.png](https://github.com/SamuelEnrique/erw/blob/main/docs/reviews/ruling-alternatives/impact-5-table-1280.png); the page today: [impact-5-default-1280.png](https://github.com/SamuelEnrique/erw/blob/main/docs/reviews/ruling-alternatives/impact-5-default-1280.png), [impact-5-table-default-1280.png](https://github.com/SamuelEnrique/erw/blob/main/docs/reviews/ruling-alternatives/impact-5-table-default-1280.png)
- **Recommendation:** **no basis to recommend**. The statute's reach over electrical materials has not been read, and their share of the purchases is not held, so neither a rule nor a figure decides. To weigh: the other choice can only be shown as a bound until both are read.
- **Figures from:** [the measurement, Local impact](https://github.com/SamuelEnrique/erw/blob/main/docs/reviews/ruling-impact-impact-2026-11-17.md), [the record of the branches, Local impact](https://github.com/SamuelEnrique/erw/blob/main/docs/reviews/ruling-alternatives-impact-2026-11-17.md)
- **Evidence (looked up on 19 November 2026):**
  - **Published practice:** Two Wisconsin documents, read today, give the same list. (1) Wisconsin Administrative Code, Tax 11.10, "Wind, solar, and certain gas powered products", the Department of Revenue's rule under Statutes 77.54(56) (https://docs.legis.wisconsin.gov/code/admin_code/tax/11/III/10), subsections (3) and (4). Exempt as part of the product: photovoltaic cells, modules and arrays with their tracking equipment; inverters, with the property that carries the direct current from the product to the inverter; and the hardware needed to install these. Not exempt: a foundation; property that stores the electricity; and, except for that direct current run to the inverter, "property necessary to convey, transfer, or alter" the electricity the product makes. (2) Wisconsin Department of Revenue, Publication 207, "Sales and Use Tax Information for Contractors" (1/19 edition, https://www.revenue.wi.gov/DOR%20Publications/pb207.pdf), the section on wind, gas and solar powered products: the same two lists, in nearly the same words. So the state's own rule exempts the mounting (as installation hardware and tracking), the modules and the inverters, and taxes the electrical materials that carry the alternating current away (collection cable, transformers), with one small exception: the direct current wiring between the modules and the inverter is exempt. That wiring's share of JEDI's electrical line is not held, so the exempt share is a little above 83.6 percent and far below 100.
  - **Samuel's own rulings on a like question:** None found. Searched `docs/state/rulings_2026-10-11.md` (no ruling in it), `docs/state/rulings_2026-11-06.md`, the section "Rulings" of `docs/roadmap.md` and the page standard (rule 9 of `queue/done/302-day-16-november-oldlaptop-large-load-on-the-site.md`) for exemption, sales tax, share and upper bound. The page standard's "real numbers only, never a placeholder" bears on the other choice, which can only be drawn as a bound (the electrical materials' qualifying share is not held).
  - **The tool's own method note:** `docs/methods/forecast.md`, section 5, the 25 states read in session 6051: for solar the machinery held is the modules and inverters, 67.2 percent of JEDI's taxable purchases, and "Wisconsin names the trackers too, 83.6 percent"; the hover names the exemption and says the tax shown is an upper bound. The entry in `site/data/forecast/sales_exemptions.json` (Wisconsin, solar) says foundations and collection equipment are taxed.
- **Recommendation after the evidence:** **keep**. The Department of Revenue's own rule (Tax 11.10(4)(c)) and its Publication 207 say the property that conveys, transfers or alters the electricity is not part of the exempt product, which is the electrical materials the other choice would exempt.

`Ruling:`

- **Figures from:** [the measurement, Local impact](https://github.com/SamuelEnrique/erw/blob/main/docs/reviews/ruling-impact-impact-2026-11-17.md), [the record of the branches, Local impact](https://github.com/SamuelEnrique/erw/blob/main/docs/reviews/ruling-alternatives-impact-2026-11-17.md)
- **Evidence (looked up on 19 November 2026):**
  - **Published practice:** The two states' own publishers answer differently, both read today. Missouri: the Department of Revenue's Letter Ruling 8180, "Taxability of Machinery & Equipment for Solar Electric Facility", 28 January 2022 (https://dor.mo.gov/rulings/show/8180), issue 3: the materials and supplies that make the foundation under the module supports are not exempt under section 144.030.2(5), because they are "not used directly in manufacturing"; issue 4 says the same of the combining equipment, cables and conduits. The Department's regulation 12 CSR 10-111.010 (Code of State Regulations, https://www.sos.mo.gov/cmsimages/adrules/csr/current/12csr/12c10-111.pdf) does exempt materials solely required to install exempt machinery, but its own case notes record Empire District Electric Co. v. Director of Revenue (1983), where concrete used at a generating plant for duct banks was held taxable. So in Missouri the tax office rules that foundations are not exempt. California: the Department of Tax and Fee Administration's Regulation 1525.4 (https://www.cdtfa.ca.gov/lawguides/vol1/sutr/1525-4.html), subdivision (b), the definition of qualified tangible personal property, item 4: since 1 January 2018 it includes "special purpose buildings and foundations" used as an integral part of generating, storing or distributing electric power, and only those built for specific machinery and used for nothing else; general purpose buildings are left out. So in California some foundations do qualify, by a test the tool cannot apply to a default plant, and no publisher gives their share of a plant's purchases. Neither state's rule exempts every taxable purchase, which is what the other choice draws (Missouri wind at 100 percent, California's points on every purchase).
  - **Samuel's own rulings on a like question:** None found. Searched `docs/state/rulings_2026-10-11.md` (no ruling in it), `docs/state/rulings_2026-11-06.md`, the section "Rulings" of `docs/roadmap.md` and the page standard (rule 9 of `queue/done/302-day-16-november-oldlaptop-large-load-on-the-site.md`) for exemption, foundation, machinery and upper bound. The page standard's "real numbers only, never a placeholder" bears on the other choice: the foundations' share is not held, so it can be drawn only at a bound.
  - **The tool's own method note:** `docs/methods/forecast.md`, section 5, the 25 states read in session 6051: most states reach a plant through a machinery exemption, which exempts the generating machinery "and not the construction materials"; the machinery held is the modules and inverters, the turbines and blades, or the power generation equipment; the hover names the exemption and says the tax shown is an upper bound. The Missouri entries of `site/data/forecast/sales_exemptions.json` already cite Letter Ruling 8180 for "not foundation materials"; the California entries say foundations and special purpose buildings qualify too, so the tax shown is an upper bound.
- **Recommendation after the evidence:** **keep**. Missouri's Department of Revenue ruled in Letter Ruling 8180 that foundation materials are not exempt, and for California, where special purpose foundations do qualify but no share is held, the method note's rule (count the generating machinery and say the tax is an upper bound) is the one that gives a figure and not a bound.

`Ruling:`

- **Change:** With a levy typed: solar -66.8 percent, wind -77.6 percent, the gas plant -89.0 percent. As the page opens (no levy) only the gas production tax moves.

### 37. The datacenter tracker, the views by operator: Count a facility under its developer when no operator is named

- **Page:** The datacenter tracker, the views by operator, https://erw-flame.vercel.app/datacenters
- **Question:** The views by operator put a facility with no named operator under "Operator not stated", even where its developer is named. Should a facility count under its developer when no operator is named? (The tracker is held for a license today, so no visitor sees these views either way.)
- **Now:** The operator alone: "Operator not stated" holds 13 facilities that state megawatts, 26,124 MW, 92.5 percent of all the megawatts stated.
- **Other choice:** Operator or developer: "Operator not stated" holds 4 facilities and 5,420 MW; 9 facilities move to their developers' lines, which enter the top of the chart. The facilities counted (274) and the total megawatts (28,254) do not move.
- **Change:** "Operator not stated" -20,704 MW, -79.3 percent.
- **Branch:** [wip/alt-datacenters-2](https://github.com/SamuelEnrique/erw/tree/wip/alt-datacenters-2). One older test that holds the operator-alone figures is left failing on the branch; it would move with the ruling.
- **Screenshots:** the other choice: [datacenters-2-operator-or-developer-held-1280.png](https://github.com/SamuelEnrique/erw/blob/main/docs/reviews/ruling-alternatives/datacenters-2-operator-or-developer-held-1280.png); the page today: screenshot not taken yet
- **Recommendation:** **no basis to recommend**. No allowed ground decides it. To weigh: the warehouse's own rollup table already counts operator or developer, so the page and the table differ today; a developer is not always the company that will run the site.
- **Figures from:** [the measurement, datacenter tracker](https://github.com/SamuelEnrique/erw/blob/main/docs/reviews/ruling-impact-datacenters-2026-11-17.md), [the record of the branches, datacenter tracker](https://github.com/SamuelEnrique/erw/blob/main/docs/reviews/ruling-alternatives-datacenters-2026-11-17.md)
- **Evidence (looked up on 19 November 2026):**
  - **Published practice:** The two publishers read keep a site's roles apart and do not fill one from another. Epoch AI's dataset of AI data centers (https://epoch.ai/data/data-centers, the explorer and the notes under it) holds the owner and the primary user as separate fields with a filter each; a site with no known owner reads "Unknown", and an affiliation that is not well evidenced is marked likely or speculative, not moved to another field. EIA does the same for power plants: the Form EIA-860 detailed data (https://www.eia.gov/electricity/data/eia860/, the list of files) holds the owners in a file of their own, the Owner file, for generators owned in shares or owned whole by someone other than the operator, and says a generator not in that file is owned by its operator; operator and owner stay two fields. Neither counts a facility under a second role when the first is not named.
  - **Samuel's own rulings on a like question:** None found on operator or developer (searched `docs/state/rulings_2026-10-11.md`, `docs/state/rulings_2026-11-06.md`, the roadmap's rulings and the page standard). The nearest is the human ruling at the head of `archive/sessions/SESSION_16_PROMPT.md` (late September 2026): the extractor does not infer beyond the stated words, and empty is correct. A developer named in a story is not stated to be the operator.
  - **The tool's own method note:** The two notes differ and leave it open. `docs/methods/datacenter_tracker.md`, section 3 ("Who the megawatts belong to"), decision 9 and section 13, item 5: the views by operator read the operator a source names; the developer is shown in the facility's own row; whether the views should read the developer is the owner's to rule. `docs/methods/datacenter_tracker_rollups.md`: the warehouse's rollup table reads the operator, else the developer.
- **Recommendation after the evidence:** **keep**. Published practice decided it: Epoch AI and EIA both keep the roles in separate fields and leave an unnamed one unknown, and Samuel's ruling of session 16 (nothing inferred beyond the stated words) points the same way; the tracker's own two notes differ, so they do not decide. A view by developer of its own would be a third choice that the branch does not build.

`Ruling:`

### 44. Power prices, the Southwest Power Pool's hubs, and the network page's price: Name the Southwest Power Pool's real-time table

- **Page:** Power prices, the Southwest Power Pool's hubs, and the network page's price, https://erw-flame.vercel.app/prices
- **Question:** The site's list of markets names no real-time table for the Southwest Power Pool, so its hub pages show day-ahead prices only and the network page shows its day-ahead price. Should the real-time table be named?
- **Now:** Not named. The pool's North hub, day-ahead, over the hours both markets hold in the last 30 days: USD 28.33 per MWh; the South hub 20.68. The latest hour held (13 November, 05:00 UTC), day-ahead: North 34.61, South 24.93.
- **Other choice:** Named. The network page's price for the pool becomes real time: the latest hour North 21.12, South 14.63; over the same hours of the 30 days North 26.13, South 20.06. Each hub page gains the real-time history held (768 hours, 24 September to 13 November 2026).
- **Change:** The network page's latest price: North -39.0 percent, South -41.3 percent in that hour; over the 30 days -7.8 and -3.0 percent. A real-time history is added to two hub pages.
- **Branch:** [wip/alt-prices-4](https://github.com/SamuelEnrique/erw/tree/wip/alt-prices-4)
- **Screenshots:** screenshot not taken yet
- **Recommendation:** **no basis to recommend**. No allowed ground decides it. To weigh: the real-time table is public and already in the warehouse; one hour's real-time price can sit far from day-ahead (from -61.48 to +177.51 in the 30 days), so the network page's figure for this grid would move more from hour to hour.
- **Figures from:** [the measurement, power prices](https://github.com/SamuelEnrique/erw/blob/main/docs/reviews/ruling-impact-prices-2026-11-17.md), [the record of the branches, power prices](https://github.com/SamuelEnrique/erw/blob/main/docs/reviews/ruling-alternatives-prices-2026-11-17.md)
- **Evidence (looked up on 19 November 2026):**
  - **Published practice:** The publishers read show both markets and none leaves a grid's real-time price out where the operator publishes one. The Independent Market Monitor for ERCOT (Potomac Economics), "2021 State of the Market Report for the ERCOT Electricity Markets" (https://www.potomaceconomics.com/wp-content/uploads/2022/05/2021-State-of-the-Market-Report.pdf): the real-time price is the market's price of record (Table 1, "Average Annual Real-Time Energy Market Prices by Zone", PDF page 44 of 211), with day-ahead set beside it as a question of convergence (Figures 28 and 29). EIA, "Wholesale Electricity Market Portal" (https://www.eia.gov/electricity/wholesalemarkets/): sends the reader to each operator's own price data, the Southwest Power Pool's among the seven, and chooses no market. The Southwest Power Pool's own pages were not read today (its site has no robots file, HTTP 404, and nothing further was asked of it).
  - **Samuel's own rulings on a like question:** None found on naming the table (searched the two rulings files, the roadmap's "Rulings" and the page standard for SPP, real time and markets.json). Two that touch it and do not bar it: the roadmap's line of 12 October 2026, "NYISO and SPP tables stay held until their licenses are answered", answered the question on the two reserve price tables (items B3 and L1 of `docs/state/rulings_2026-10-11.md`); since then `docs/licenses.md` (13 November 2026) reads the pool's terms as "may be public", holds `iso_rtm_hub_prices` for MISO's rows alone, and the price board draws the pool's real-time rows. His ruling of 20 October 2026 (`docs/methods/power_prices.md`, "The rules") keeps the day-ahead column on the prices page, and it stays under both choices.
  - **The tool's own method note:** `docs/methods/grid_network_v3.md`, "Prices", and `docs/methods/grid_network.md` ("Prices, and what the ring does not say"): the network's price is "the real-time price at the grid's main hub", drawn wherever a public one is held. `docs/methods/power_prices.md`, "A hub's page": a hub's page draws real time where `site/data/markets.json` names a table, and the live set has held the pool's 15-minute real-time hub prices since 24 September 2026; the note leaves the naming to this ruling. `docs/methods/price_board.md` already draws the pool's main hub in both markets. The market file's own reason for the gap ("SPP has no real-time series table in the ERW") has not been true since 24 September. To know before landing: the browser read of the month branch found the pool's real-time month line blank for the same missing name, and one hour's real-time price moves more than day-ahead (from -61.48 to +177.51 against day-ahead in the 30 days).
- **Recommendation after the evidence:** **switch**. The network page's own method note decided it: its price is the real-time price at a grid's main hub wherever a public one is held, and the pool's has been held, public and drawn on the price board since 24 September 2026.

### 46. Shoulder hours, the ten worst days: Rank the ten worst days by the tightest evening

- **Page:** Shoulder hours, the ten worst days, https://erw-flame.vercel.app/shoulder?grid=ercot
- **Question:** "The ten worst days of 2025" are the days with the largest swing above the day's own average. Should they be the days with the tightest evening instead, the highest evening net load?
- **Now:** By energy above the day's average. Texas 2025: ten days from October to February, evening peaks 40,269 to 58,028 MW (mean 48,751 MW), mean 101,073 MWh above the average, mean 8.88 hours needed.
- **Other choice:** By the evening's highest net load: ten days of July to early September (18 August 2025 first, 68,781 MW), mean peak 67,122 MW, mean 64,206 MWh above the average, mean 5.78 hours needed. No day is in both lists. California's ten were not ranked (its data file on the measuring machine was empty).
- **Change:** All ten days change; mean evening peak +37.7 percent, mean energy above the average -36.5 percent, mean hours needed -3.10, -34.9 percent.
- **Branch:** [wip/alt-shoulder-4](https://github.com/SamuelEnrique/erw/tree/wip/alt-shoulder-4)
- **Screenshots:** screenshot not taken yet
- **Recommendation:** **no basis to recommend**. What "worst" means is not fixed by a published figure, a ruling or the method note beyond the present definition. To weigh: the present list is ten mild-season days with low peaks and long swings; the other is the ten summer evenings when the grid was tightest, which is the usual meaning of a worst day for reliability.
- **Figures from:** [the measurement, shoulder hours](https://github.com/SamuelEnrique/erw/blob/main/docs/reviews/ruling-impact-shoulder-2026-11-17.md), [the record of the branches, shoulder hours](https://github.com/SamuelEnrique/erw/blob/main/docs/reviews/ruling-alternatives-shoulder-2026-11-17.md)
- **Evidence (looked up on 19 November 2026):**
  - **Published practice:** Three publishers read; each picks its hardest days or hours by the level of load or net load, and none by a swing around the day's own mean. CAISO's Department of Market Monitoring, *2024 Special Report on Battery Storage* (29 May 2025, https://www.caiso.com/documents/2024-special-report-on-battery-storage-may-29-2025.pdf, page 30, PDF page 31): to judge what the battery fleet did in the most critical evening hours it takes "the five days with the highest average daily load". The same department's *2025 Annual Report on Market Issues and Performance* (June 2026, https://www.caiso.com/documents/2025-annual-report-on-market-issues-and-performance.pdf, page 313, PDF page 325) picks its strained days by the highest load forecast, the day-ahead energy price and the scarcity of capacity. Potomac Economics, *2025 State of the Market Report for the ERCOT Electricity Markets* (June 2026, https://www.potomaceconomics.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/06/2025-State-of-the-Market-Report-for-ERCOT.pdf, page 5): conditions are tightest at the evening net load peak; page 116: its tight hours are those with net load in the highest quarter of the year, "when supply was most likely to be tight". The National Renewable Energy Laboratory (*The Potential for Battery Energy Storage to Provide Peaking Capacity in the United States*, NREL/TP-6A20-74184, June 2019, https://docs.nlr.gov/docs/fy19osti/74184.pdf, PDF page 10) says a resource's worth at the peak can be read from its output in "the highest net load hours of the year". They agree on level and differ on which one: the day's average load in California's monitor, net load in Texas's and the laboratory's. The other choice, the evening's highest net load, is Texas's monitor's.
  - **Samuel's own rulings on a like question:** None found (searched `docs/state/rulings_2026-10-11.md`, `docs/state/rulings_2026-11-06.md`, the roadmap's "Rulings" and the page standard for worst, ten days, rank and tight).
  - **The tool's own method note:** `docs/methods/shoulder_hours.md`, "The second measure and the worst days" (session 80): each complete day is measured against its own mean, and the ten days of a year with the most shoulder energy above that mean are ranked. The same note, "What it cannot see", says what a worst day is in words: what the average day smooths away, "the cloudy week, the calm evening and the heat wave". The present ten of Texas's 2025 are all from October to February, with evening peaks of 40,269 to 58,028 MW; none is a summer day, where the other choice's ten are (July to early September, 18 August first at 68,781 MW). `docs/methods/shoulder.md`, section 5 (key `worst-days`), gives the present rule and no reason for the swing over the level.
- **Recommendation after the evidence:** **switch**. Published practice decided it: both market monitors and the laboratory choose their hardest days and hours by the level of load or net load, none by the swing around a day's own mean, and the method note's own picture of a worst day (the heat wave, the calm evening) is of such days; which level (the evening's highest net load, as built) stays part of the ruling.

`Ruling:`

### 47. What a generator earns, a contracted plant's worst month: The worst month over every year held, or the last few

- **Page:** What a generator earns, a contracted plant's worst month, https://erw-flame.vercel.app/cost-of-power/seller?mw=200&bn=100&bp=38
- **Question:** "The worst month" of a plant with a contract is the lowest month of every month held since July 2018. Should it be the lowest of the last few years only, as a lender may look at it?
- **Now:** Every month held (99). A 200 MW solar plant with all its energy contracted at USD 38 per MWh: December 2018, USD 750,064, debt coverage 0.46 times.
- **Other choice:** The last three years (36 months): December 2025, USD 1,016,595, coverage 0.69 times. Over five years: December 2022, USD 783,506, 0.49 times. The plant without a contract does not move: February 2024 is its worst month in every window.
- **Change:** +266,531 USD, +35.5 percent; coverage +0.23 (three years). +4.5 percent over five years.
- **Branch:** [wip/alt-seller-2](https://github.com/SamuelEnrique/erw/tree/wip/alt-seller-2). The branch takes three years; five is one constant away.
- **Screenshots:** the other choice: [seller-2-contract-lowest-month-1280.png](https://github.com/SamuelEnrique/erw/blob/main/docs/reviews/ruling-alternatives/seller-2-contract-lowest-month-1280.png); the page today: screenshot not taken yet
- **Recommendation:** **no basis to recommend**. No allowed ground says how far back a worst month should look. To weigh: the longer window is the more cautious figure and uses every month held; how many years "the last few" is (three or five) would be part of the ruling.
- **Figures from:** [the measurement, generator page](https://github.com/SamuelEnrique/erw/blob/main/docs/reviews/ruling-impact-seller-2026-11-17.md), [the record of the branches, generator page](https://github.com/SamuelEnrique/erw/blob/main/docs/reviews/ruling-alternatives-seller-2026-11-17.md)
- **Evidence (looked up on 19 November 2026):**
  - **Published practice:** The publishers do not make this choice: none prints a plant's worst month, so none says how far back it should look. Potomac Economics, *2025 State of the Market Report for the ERCOT Electricity Markets* (June 2026, https://www.potomaceconomics.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/06/2025-State-of-the-Market-Report-for-ERCOT.pdf, pages 97 and 100 to 102): what a new gas, wind or solar plant could have earned is given by calendar year, each year beside the years before it, with the remark that years of lower net revenue "occur often". The Annual Technology Baseline 2025, "Financial Cases and Methods" (https://atb.nlr.gov/electricity/2025/financial_cases_&_methods): a required debt service coverage ratio sets how much debt a plant carries; the page names no month, no worst month and no look-back window. Lazard, *Levelized Cost of Energy+* (2026 edition, https://www.lazard.com/media/kcfconhf/lazards-lcoeplus_vf.pdf, PDF page 25, the sample calculation): one figure a year over the life, no month. A lender's or a rating agency's own test was not looked for: none is among the publishers this page's readers were named to trust, so "as a lender may look at it" stands unread.
  - **Samuel's own rulings on a like question:** None found (searched `docs/state/rulings_2026-10-11.md`, `docs/state/rulings_2026-11-06.md`, the roadmap's "Rulings" and the page standard for worst month, lowest month, bad month, lender and window). His ruling of 12 October 2026 in the roadmap (the battery headline counts whole months only) is about which months are whole, not how many years a worst month looks over.
  - **The tool's own method note:** `docs/methods/generator_earns_algorithm.md`, section 11 ("The worst month: the lowest month of every month held") and the section of sessions 7520 and 7523 ("A contract's own worst month": the lowest of the months held): the present rule, which the row's own label states. The read behind that section (`docs/reviews/generator-read-2026-11-16.md`, row 7) marks the window as the owner's to rule and adds that under a contract the worst month is the fleet's least productive month of 2018, on that year's fleet, not a new plant's. The sister page's note differs: `docs/methods/battery_earns_algorithm.md`, section 4.3, takes its "bad month" from the 36 months to the newest (their tenth percentile, not their lowest). So the site's two notes hold two windows, and neither gives a reason that would bind the other.
- **Recommendation after the evidence:** **no basis to recommend**. Looked for a published rule on a worst month's window in the market monitor's report, the Annual Technology Baseline and Lazard and found none; no ruling of Samuel's touches it; and the generator's note (every month held) and the battery's note (36 months) state different windows for the like figure, so the notes do not decide either.

`Ruling:`

### 48. Automated Analysis, "Today's chart" of 12 November 2026: Correct the stored chart: the record was July 2026's

- **Page:** Automated Analysis, "Today's chart" of 12 November 2026, https://erw-flame.vercel.app/research
- **Question:** The stored chart of 12 November says Wyoming's gas-fired generation in August 2026 broke a record set in July 2024. July 2026 had already broken it. Should a correction be added to the stored chart?
- **Now:** No correction. The stored text reads: "The record until now was 765,381 MWh, in July 2024".
- **Other choice:** A correction line under the stored record: the record until now was 1,034,451 MWh, in July 2026, and August 2026 (1,060,504 MWh) was above it by 26,053 MWh, 2.5 percent. The stored chart and its headline ("the highest monthly value held since January 2001", which stands) are left as stored.
- **Change:** The record named: 765,381 to 1,034,451 MWh, +269,069 MWh (269,069.43 on the table's values; 269,070 is the two rounded figures' difference), +35.2 percent.
- **Branch:** [wip/alt-research-2](https://github.com/SamuelEnrique/erw/tree/wip/alt-research-2)
- **Screenshots:** the other choice: [research-2-chart-correction-1280.png](https://github.com/SamuelEnrique/erw/blob/main/docs/reviews/ruling-alternatives/research-2-chart-correction-1280.png); the page today: screenshot not taken yet
- **Recommendation:** **no basis to recommend**. No recommendation: the measurement and the build give one figure of this line differently (see the foot). What would otherwise bear on it: a published figure shows the stored sentence is wrong, EIA's own July 2026 value for Wyoming (1,034,451 MWh on the data machine's copy; 1,028,747 on an earlier vintage), either of which is above the 765,381 MWh the sentence names. Which vintage the correction names would be part of the ruling.
- **Figures from:** [the measurement, Automated Analysis](https://github.com/SamuelEnrique/erw/blob/main/docs/reviews/ruling-impact-research-2026-11-17.md), [the record of the branches, Automated Analysis](https://github.com/SamuelEnrique/erw/blob/main/docs/reviews/ruling-alternatives-research-2026-11-17.md)
- **Evidence (looked up on 19 November 2026):**
  - **Published practice:** EIA, the publisher of the figure, corrects what it has already published and says so. Its Information Quality Guidelines (https://www.eia.gov/about/information_quality_guidelines.php, section 4a on objectivity): when a substantive error is found after a product is out, EIA corrects it and issues "an errata notice or other notification"; preliminary and revised data are marked as such. The page does not say whether the first text is replaced or annotated. A second publisher, the Associated Press's corrections rule, could not be fetched and was not read today.
  - **Samuel's own rulings on a like question:** No ruling names a correction to a stored chart (searched `docs/state/rulings_2026-10-11.md`, `docs/state/rulings_2026-11-06.md`, the roadmap's rulings and the page standard for correct, errata, stored and record). Two standing rules bear on it. `CLAUDE.md`, non-negotiables 1 and 5: real data only, and every number traces to its source; the stored sentence names a record that EIA's own July 2026 figure had already passed. `CLAUDE.md`, non-negotiable 3: an existing file is not deleted or overwritten, it is extended; the other choice leaves the stored record as it is and adds a line under it. The ruling of 12 October 2026, night (`docs/roadmap.md`), makes the daily chart automatic with no approval; it says nothing on mending one.
  - **The tool's own method note:** `docs/methods/automated_analysis_scanner.md`, "Record high or low", already states the rule and names this case: since session 1982 (13 November 2026) "the record until now" is the newer value when a month of the same fresh window had already beaten the held record, and the Digest of 12 November 2026 is given as the fault that led to it: August 2026 (1,060,504 MWh) was said to break July 2024's 765,381 MWh when July 2026 (1,034,451 MWh) had broken it first. So the note gives the corrected record and its figure; only the stored chart still reads the old one.
- **Recommendation after the evidence:** **switch**. The method note decided it: by the tool's own rule the record until now was July 2026's 1,034,451 MWh, the note names the stored chart of 12 November as wrong, and EIA's practice and non-negotiable 3 both fit a correction added under the record with nothing overwritten; still open, and not settled by this, are the 1 MWh by which the list's two files differ on the old margin (1,060,504 less 765,381 is 295,123) and the earlier vintage that reads July 2026 as 1,028,747 MWh.

`Ruling:`

### 49. Demand growth, the weather view: Fit the weather view on 2019 to 2024, not 2019 to 2021

- **Page:** Demand growth, the weather view, https://erw-flame.vercel.app/demand
- **Question:** The weather view fits demand to weather on 2019 to 2021 and calls later growth the weather does not explain a "finding". The hottest summer peaks of 2025 and 2026 lie outside that fit's weather, so they cannot be called findings. Should the fit use 2019 to 2024?
- **Now:** Fit on 2019 to 2021. Texas's 2026 summer peak: +15.37 percent unexplained, not a finding (hotter than any hour of the fit); its 2025 peak +12.72 against an uncertainty of 6.32, a finding. Texas's 2025 energy: +23.86 unexplained (uncertainty 4.09). California's 2025 energy +4.88 (2.90), a finding.
- **Other choice:** Fit on 2019 to 2024. Texas's 2026 peak +11.89 against 9.88, a finding; its 2025 peak +9.64 against 9.88, no longer one. Texas's 2025 energy +16.25 (13.23). California's 2025 energy +3.31 (3.94), no longer a finding. The fit's error on Texas hours it has not seen rises from 4.19 to 7.71 percent, since 2022 to 2024 carry growth the fit takes for weather.
- **Change:** Texas's unexplained 2025 energy growth -7.61 points (-31.9 percent of the figure) and its uncertainty three times wider; the "finding" marks change both ways (2 of 8 summer peaks marked under each rule, different ones).
- **Branch:** [wip/alt-demand-1](https://github.com/SamuelEnrique/erw/tree/wip/alt-demand-1)
- **Screenshots:** screenshot not taken yet
- **Recommendation:** **no basis to recommend**. No allowed ground decides the fit years. To weigh: the longer fit lets the hottest peaks be tested, but it absorbs three years of growth into the weather's effect, nearly doubles the fit's error and widens every uncertainty.
- **Figures from:** [the measurement, demand growth](https://github.com/SamuelEnrique/erw/blob/main/docs/reviews/ruling-impact-demand-2026-11-17.md), [the record of the branches, demand growth](https://github.com/SamuelEnrique/erw/blob/main/docs/reviews/ruling-alternatives-demand-2026-11-17.md)
- **Evidence (looked up on 19 November 2026):**
  - **Published practice:** The one operator read does not make this choice, and no second publisher was read. ERCOT, "2025 Long-Term Hourly Peak Demand and Energy Forecast" (8 April 2025, https://www.ercot.com/files/docs/2025/04/08/ERCOT-2025-Long-Term-Load-Forecast-Report.pdf), pages 12 and 13, "Hourly Demand Models" and "Model Building Process": ERCOT fits its hourly demand models on January 2019 to May 2024, "only five years of historical data", but with the number of premises as a term that carries the growth, and it gets normal weather by running the weather of 2008 to 2024 through the model. That is a forecast with a growth term, not a measure of growth against a fixed base whose fit has no trend, so it does not say which years this view's fit should stand on. NERC's site was not read (its robots file refuses the reader's agents). EIA's Short-Term Energy Outlook was not read today for its weather normals.
  - **Samuel's own rulings on a like question:** The page standard (rule 9 of `queue/done/302-day-16-november-oldlaptop-large-load-on-the-site.md`, 16 November 2026): an upgrade "drops nothing the page had". The other choice puts 2022 to 2024 inside the fit, and the record of the branch says those three years are then no longer reported on the view. Nothing on the fit's years in `docs/state/rulings_2026-10-11.md`, `docs/state/rulings_2026-11-06.md` or the roadmap's "Rulings" (searched: weather, fit, 2021, finding, normal).
  - **The tool's own method note:** `docs/methods/demand_weather.md`, "What it answers" and "The figures": the view is defined as each year after 2021 against the average of 2019 to 2021, the fit standing for what the customers of 2019 to 2021 would have used in that year's weather, with "no trend" in the fit. "The uncertainty" and "What is not in it" state as a limit, not a fault, that a peak hotter than every hour of 2019 to 2021 is marked and is not a finding. The measurement shows what the note's design guards against: on 2019 to 2024 the fit's error on Texas hours it has not seen rises from 4.19 to 7.71 percent, because the later years' growth is read as weather.
- **Recommendation after the evidence:** **keep**. The method note decides it: the years 2019 to 2021 are the base the view is defined on and the fit has no growth term, so a fit on 2019 to 2024 would read growth as weather, and by Samuel's page standard the view would also drop the 2022 to 2024 figures it has.

`Ruling:`
