### 82. The Flex Alert scorecard: Value the 2018 to 2022 alert days at a daily price index

- **Page:** The Flex Alert scorecard, https://erw-flame.vercel.app/grid/caiso/alerts
- **Question:** Most alert days have an estimate of the energy saved but no dollar value, because hourly prices are not held for 2018 to 2022. Once a daily on-peak price index is held for those years, should those days be valued at it, as a second, labelled figure?
- **Now:** An hour is valued at its own day-ahead price, or reads "no price". 34 of the 38 alert days with an estimate read "no price" (all of 2018 to 2022); only the 4 days of 2023 and 2024 are valued.
- **Other choice:** Each of the 34 days gains one cell: its estimated reduction times the day's index. The reductions to be valued: 2018 -14,447.98 MWh, 2019 -12,697.84, 2020 7,299.55, 2021 -1,898.55, 2022 -37,495.28.
- **Change:** 34 cells added; their dollar values are not computed.
- **Branch:** not built: the index is data the warehouse does not hold (a request to the data machine is open for it).
- **Screenshots:** screenshot not taken yet
- **Recommendation:** **no basis to recommend**. No allowed ground decides it, and nothing can be shown until the index is held. To weigh: the index would value a day at one price, where the four later days are valued hour by hour, so the two kinds of figure must be labelled apart.
- **Figures from:** [the measurement, Flex Alert scorecard](https://github.com/SamuelEnrique/erw/blob/main/docs/reviews/ruling-impact-caiso-alerts-2026-11-17.md), [the record of the branch, Flex Alert scorecard](https://github.com/SamuelEnrique/erw/blob/main/docs/reviews/ruling-alternatives-caiso-alerts-2026-11-17.md)
- **Evidence (looked up on 19 November 2026):**
  - **Published practice:** The one publisher read does not make this choice: it states an alert's saving in megawatts and puts no price on it. CAISO, "First summer report on market performance released" (9 August 2021, https://www.caiso.com/about/news/energy-matters-blog/first-summer-report-on-market-performance-released): the Flex Alerts of June 2021 brought "hourly load reductions from 77 to 735" MW in the peak hours, with no dollar value. No evaluation by the California Public Utilities Commission that values an alert day at a price was found or read today, so no publisher was found to value an alert day at a daily index or at any price. On the index itself: EIA's robots file, read today (https://www.eia.gov/robots.txt), disallows every "archive" path, which is where EIA keeps the yearly files of ICE's daily prices before 2026; they were not asked.
  - **Samuel's own rulings on a like question:** No ruling decides it, and two of his standing rules make it his call alone. The index is ICE's data, held internal (`docs/state/data_requests_done_line.md`, row 200, answered by session 7052: not fetched, since EIA's robots file bars the archive path; a person could download the 2018 to 2025 files by hand, and the table would stay internal). The warehouse already holds that index from 29 December 2025, so the earlier years are more of a source already held, not a new one; what bars them is the robots file and the license. The page standard (rule 9 of `queue/done/302-day-16-november-oldlaptop-large-load-on-the-site.md`): a held or internal value is drawn blank with "license needed" on hover, so 34 cells computed from an internal index would raise the open question of whether a figure computed from a held price is held too. Ruling 2 of 6 November 2026 (`docs/state/rulings_2026-11-06.md`) keeps a robots refusal for every host. Searched also `docs/state/rulings_2026-10-11.md` for: Flex Alert, index, ICE, value.
  - **The tool's own method note:** `docs/methods/flex_alert_scorecard.md`, section 7, "Value": a day's value is the sum over its alert hours of the reduction times that hour's day-ahead price, the mean of the two California hubs, and "an hour counts only with both hubs". `docs/methods/caiso_alerts.md`, section 7, lists "a value before July 2023" among what the tool does not hold, because the operator's price service reaches back about 39 months. The note's rule is hour by hour; it neither allows nor forbids a second, labelled figure at a daily price.
- **Recommendation after the evidence:** **no basis to recommend**. Looked for and not found: a publisher that values an alert day at a daily price index, or a ruling on a second figure from ICE's prices; it turns on a license (ICE's data, held internal) and on files EIA's robots file bars a machine from fetching, which are Samuel's alone to rule.

`Ruling:`

### 83. What Texas's batteries were awarded day-ahead: Give the gap's three parts month by month

- **Page:** What Texas's batteries were awarded day-ahead, https://erw-flame.vercel.app/cost-of-power/battery/awards
- **Question:** The gap between the model and the awards is split into three parts (never offered, offered and not awarded, price) for the months together. Should the parts also be given month by month?
- **Now:** Over January to July 2026 together: gap USD 23.61 per kW; never offered 9.70 (41.1 percent), offered and not awarded 14.53 (61.5 percent), price -0.62.
- **Other choice:** The same, and a table month by month: seven months, four cells each. Never offered runs from 34.5 percent (January) to 44.2 percent (April); not awarded from 58.8 (May) to 67.7 percent (January). July 2026: gap 2.19, parts 0.95, 1.30 and -0.06.
- **Change:** 28 cells added (the measurement counts the 21 parts; the branch also prints each month's gap); no figure that stands moves.
- **Branch:** [wip/alt-battery-awards-2](https://github.com/SamuelEnrique/erw/tree/wip/alt-battery-awards-2)
- **Screenshots:** screenshot not taken yet
- **Recommendation:** **no basis to recommend**. No allowed ground decides it. To weigh: the monthly shares stay close to the total's, so the table adds detail more than a new finding.
- **Figures from:** [the measurement, battery awards](https://github.com/SamuelEnrique/erw/blob/main/docs/reviews/ruling-impact-battery-awards-2026-11-17.md), [the record of the branches, battery awards](https://github.com/SamuelEnrique/erw/blob/main/docs/reviews/ruling-alternatives-battery-awards-2026-11-17.md)
- **Evidence (looked up on 19 November 2026):**
  - **Published practice:** The publishers do not make this choice: none splits the gap between a model and a fleet's awards, by month or at all. What Texas's market monitor does with the same services was read. Potomac Economics, *2025 State of the Market Report for the ERCOT Electricity Markets* (June 2026, https://www.potomaceconomics.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/06/2025-State-of-the-Market-Report-for-ERCOT.pdf, pages 39 and 40, Figures 24 and 25): the ancillary services' quantities and day-ahead prices are shown "on a monthly basis for 2025" and by year for 2021 to 2025, months beside years; its storage revenue (pages 22 and 23) is by year only. CAISO's Department of Market Monitoring gives its model battery's revenue by quarter (*2025 Annual Report on Market Issues and Performance*, June 2026, https://www.caiso.com/documents/2025-annual-report-on-market-issues-and-performance.pdf, page 119), for another grid. So months beside a total is a form the monitor uses, but for measured figures, not for parts that are an allocation.
  - **Samuel's own rulings on a like question:** None found on the gap or its parts (searched `docs/state/rulings_2026-10-11.md`, `docs/state/rulings_2026-11-06.md`, the roadmap's "Rulings" and the page standard for awards, gap, offered and month by month). Two of his rules sit near it and pull apart. The roadmap's ruling of 12 October 2026 for the battery page, of which this is a sub-page: monthly rows go into the CSV download. The page standard (16 November 2026): two cases side by side wherever a reader would compare, and an upgrade drops nothing; the other choice adds 28 cells and removes none. The awards page's own head is already "By month" and "By year", which no ruling took off the page.
  - **The tool's own method note:** `docs/methods/battery_awards.md`, section 3: the gap is the model's total less the awards' total over the months the three tables hold whole, "the months added up"; it is silent on parts by month. `docs/methods/ercot_storage_dam_offers.md`, "The gap in three parts": "never offered" is "an allocation, not a measurement" (it takes the hours with no offer to have made what the model makes on average), and "offered and not awarded" is what is left. Section 5 of the first note: nothing published stands against the three parts. A table by month would apply the same allocation to seven shorter spans.
- **Recommendation after the evidence:** **no basis to recommend**. Looked for a publisher that splits such a gap and found none (the monitor shows measured ancillary figures by month, not allocated parts); no ruling of Samuel's covers it, and the two nearest rules of his point opposite ways; the method note states the parts for the months together and says nothing on months apart.

- **Page:** Where power is cheap (the hub comparison), https://erw-flame.vercel.app/prices/compare
- **Question:** The page compares hubs over two fixed windows: the last twelve months and the last month. Should the reader be able to choose any run of months, such as summer 2026 or calendar 2025?
- **Now:** Two windows. Average day-ahead price over the twelve months to October 2026: the Southwest Power Pool's North hub USD 34.27 per MWh, Southern California 30.27, Northern California 34.11, the Texas hub average 34.59.
- **Other choice:** A section "Any months". Calendar 2025: North hub 28.09, Southern California 32.22, Northern California 38.23, Texas 33.50. Summer 2026 (June to August): 40.17, 34.83, 35.38 and 30.96. The two fixed windows stay.
- **Change:** A section added; against the twelve months, calendar 2025 is -18.0 percent for the North hub and summer 2026 +17.2 percent; Texas's summer -10.5 percent.
- **Branch:** [wip/alt-compare-2](https://github.com/SamuelEnrique/erw/tree/wip/alt-compare-2)
- **Screenshots:** screenshot not taken yet
- **Recommendation:** **no basis to recommend**. No allowed ground decides it. To weigh: which hub is cheapest changes with the window (the North hub is the cheapest in calendar 2025 and the dearest in summer 2026), so a fixed window hides it; the data machine must run the branch's builder before the page can draw it.
- **Figures from:** [the measurement, hub comparison](https://github.com/SamuelEnrique/erw/blob/main/docs/reviews/ruling-impact-compare-2026-11-17.md), [the record of the branches, hub comparison](https://github.com/SamuelEnrique/erw/blob/main/docs/reviews/ruling-alternatives-compare-2026-11-17.md)
- **Evidence (looked up on 19 November 2026):**
  - **Published practice:** The publishers read report hub and zone prices by calendar year and calendar month, never over a rolling twelve months, and none read is a page where a reader chooses a run of months, so they do not make this choice. The Independent Market Monitor for ERCOT (Potomac Economics), "2021 State of the Market Report for the ERCOT Electricity Markets" (https://www.potomaceconomics.com/wp-content/uploads/2022/05/2021-State-of-the-Market-Report.pdf): yearly averages by zone for calendar 2014 to 2021, with one more column for 2021 without the storm's week (Table 1, PDF page 44 of 211), and monthly averages by peak and off-peak hours (Figure A1, PDF pages 157 and 158). EIA, "Wholesale Electricity and Natural Gas Market Data" (https://www.eia.gov/electricity/wholesale/): one file a calendar year, by trade date. A calendar year or a summer can be set beside these only if the page can show one; the two fixed windows cannot.
  - **Samuel's own rulings on a like question:** None found on the comparison's windows (searched the two rulings files, the roadmap's "Rulings" and the page standard for window, months and calendar). The nearest are for other pages: the roadmap's line of 12 October 2026 that every assumption shows its default and is editable in place (the economics pages' assumptions; a window is not named), and the page standard's A and B, which this page has since session 7540.
  - **The tool's own method note:** `docs/methods/hub_price_comparison.md`, "The two windows" and line 2 of "The decisions on the page": two windows, the twelve months and the last whole month, both ending with the last month every hub holds, "so no hub is compared over a month another lacks". The other choice keeps both windows and leaves a hub out of a run it does not hold whole, so it does not break that rule; the note says nothing for or against a third, chosen window. Its cross-check section says the operators' yearly reports are not yet read, and those give calendar years. The sister page already reads another window from a table (`docs/methods/datacenter_pays_algorithm.md`, section 12, line 18). The data machine must run the branch's builder before the page could draw the section.
- **Recommendation after the evidence:** **no basis to recommend**. The publishers' calendar years and months show which windows a reader would want to match, but none of them offers a chosen run of months, no ruling of Samuel's speaks of this page's windows, and the method note fixes the two windows without ruling a third out.

### 87. The three newest event pages (January 2025 cold, June 2025 heat, January 2026 cold): A temperature version for the three newest events

- **Page:** The three newest event pages (January 2025 cold, June 2025 heat, January 2026 cold), https://erw-flame.vercel.app/events/cold-2026
- **Question:** Six of the nine event pages give the extra demand both as measured and after taking out the weather. The three newest give it only as measured. Should they get a temperature version from the weather table the warehouse already holds?
- **Now:** No estimate with temperature and no weather share on the three pages; each says no weather station is held.
- **Other choice:** An estimate with temperature, from the grid's daily weather (the average of its NOAA stations, a like method on other rows than the six older pages use). PJM, extra demand a day: January 2025 412,096 MWh as measured, 90,681 with temperature (the weather explains 78.0 percent); June 2025 380,844 and 149,728 (60.7 percent); January 2026 677,815 and 154,838 (77.2 percent; its range crosses zero). Texas: 248,041 and 128,300 (48.3 percent); 346,005 and 116,860 (66.2 percent). The Southwest Power Pool: 110,081 and 72,827; 200,299 and 69,515.
- **Change:** New figures on three pages (an estimate and a weather share for each grid shown); no figure that stands moves.
- **Branch:** [wip/alt-events-3](https://github.com/SamuelEnrique/erw/tree/wip/alt-events-3). The weather table is held back from the live site's catalogue, so the pages would draw these only once the data machine serves it.
- **Screenshots:** screenshot not taken yet
- **Recommendation:** **no basis to recommend**. No allowed ground decides it. To weigh: the nine pages would then read alike; but the three would use another weather source than the six (a grid average, not the event table's own stations), and January 2026's estimate for PJM is not distinguishable from zero.
- **Figures from:** [the measurement, event pages](https://github.com/SamuelEnrique/erw/blob/main/docs/reviews/ruling-impact-events-2026-11-17.md), [the record of the branches, event pages](https://github.com/SamuelEnrique/erw/blob/main/docs/reviews/ruling-alternatives-events-2026-11-17.md)
- **Evidence (looked up on 19 November 2026):**
  - **Published practice:** No publisher read makes this choice (a named set of airports against a grid's average of stations) for a storm's demand. EIA, "Short-Term Energy Outlook Supplement: Sources of Price Volatility in the ERCOT Market", October 2022 (https://www.eia.gov/outlooks/steo/special/supplements/2022/2022_sp_03.pdf), "Modeling peak load", PDF page 17 of 26: EIA fits Texas's monthly load on heating degree days (November to April) and cooling degree days (May to October), so degree days are the publisher's weather control as they are the event pages'; it prints no storm's demand with the weather taken out. EIA's accounts of single events ("Today in Energy", 7 May 2021 and 28 September 2022) give demand as measured. FERC's and NERC's inquiry reports on the storms were not read today (FERC's page for the 2021 report answered HTTP 403 and the report's file is over the reader's size limit; NERC's robots file refuses Anthropic's agents).
  - **Samuel's own rulings on a like question:** No ruling on these three pages' weather (searched the two rulings files, the roadmap and the page standard for temperature, weather and station). Two that bear on it: ruling 1 of 6 November 2026 (`docs/state/rulings_2026-11-06.md`) allowed a further pull from a publisher already held, because "the source is the same publisher" and the "no new source" rule does not bar it; NOAA's hourly station file is already held for the six older events, so the same airports for three more windows would be a like pull, though that ruling does not name it. And his default of 18 October 2026 (`docs/methods/events.md`, "Session 308") built the January 2026 window "with the same baseline as the other eight": one method across the pages.
  - **The tool's own method note:** `docs/methods/event_study.md`, "Temperature-controlled specification", and line 12 of the decisions in `docs/methods/events.md`: the temperature version uses degree days at 65 F at each grid's named airports from the event table's own station rows (PJM: Philadelphia and Chicago O'Hare; Texas: Dallas-Fort Worth and Houston; the Southwest Power Pool: Oklahoma City), and days without those rows are left out of it. For the three newest events the notes say no station is held, so no estimate controls for temperature, and that pulling the stations would be a new pull. The other choice computes on `noaa_grid_weather_daily`, another set of stations averaged by grid, a table held back from the live site's catalogue; the note has no rule for that source, and the measurement finds January 2026's estimate for PJM not distinguishable from zero.
- **Recommendation after the evidence:** **keep**. The method note decided it: the temperature version is defined on each grid's named airports in the event table, which the three windows do not hold, and the other choice would put a second weather source on three of nine pages (ruling 1 of 6 November suggests the like-for-like way, the same airports pulled for the three windows, is open to Samuel).

- **Screenshots:** the other choice: [events-2-1280.png](https://github.com/SamuelEnrique/erw/blob/main/docs/reviews/ruling-alternatives/events-2-1280.png); the page today: [events-2-default-1280.png](https://github.com/SamuelEnrique/erw/blob/main/docs/reviews/ruling-alternatives/events-2-default-1280.png)
- **Recommendation:** **no basis to recommend**. No allowed ground decides it, and the measurement itself warns that the new figures are an extrapolation. To weigh: a reader would expect a column that controls for temperature to be lower, and here it is more than double; the page standard asks the tool to state facts.
- **Figures from:** [the measurement, event pages](https://github.com/SamuelEnrique/erw/blob/main/docs/reviews/ruling-impact-events-2026-11-17.md), [the record of the branches, event pages](https://github.com/SamuelEnrique/erw/blob/main/docs/reviews/ruling-alternatives-events-2026-11-17.md)
- **Evidence (looked up on 19 November 2026):**
  - **Published practice:** No publisher read prints a storm's demand day by day with the weather taken out. EIA, "Short-Term Energy Outlook Supplement: Sources of Price Volatility in the ERCOT Market", October 2022 (https://www.eia.gov/outlooks/steo/special/supplements/2022/2022_sp_03.pdf), "Modeling peak load", PDF page 17 of 26: load is fitted on degree days by the month, over many years, not for single days beyond the fitted range. The Independent Market Monitor for ERCOT (Potomac Economics), "2021 State of the Market Report" (https://www.potomaceconomics.com/wp-content/uploads/2022/05/2021-State-of-the-Market-Report.pdf), Table 1, PDF page 44 of 211: it sets the storm's week aside in a column of its own ("w/o Uri") and adjusts no day. FERC's and NERC's inquiry report on the December 2022 storm was not read today (FERC's site answered HTTP 403 to a report page and its report files are over the reader's size limit; NERC's robots file refuses Anthropic's agents).
  - **Samuel's own rulings on a like question:** None found on a day column (searched the two rulings files, the roadmap and the page standard for temperature, Elliott and each day). The page standard (rule 9 of `queue/done/302-day-16-november-oldlaptop-large-load-on-the-site.md`): "The tool states facts and takes no view", and real numbers only. The measurement file itself says each day's new figure is an extrapolation, not the weather's share of that day.
  - **The tool's own method note:** `docs/methods/event_study.md`, "What the pages say", and line 13 of the decisions in `docs/methods/events.md`: each page gives the two pooled estimates, and a part is put down to the weather only when the two share a sign and the one with temperature is no larger; otherwise the page says no share can be put down to weather. The days' figures fail that test where it matters: 23 December is 1,419,061 MWh with temperature against 619,176 without, 24 December 2,225,024 against 909,793. The same note calls a fit carried forward from two points "a check", "not a better estimate". The pooled figure with temperature (134,171 MWh a day) is already on the page.
- **Recommendation after the evidence:** **keep**. The method note's own condition decided it: an estimate with temperature is read as the weather's part only when it is no larger than the plain one, and the storm's coldest days come out more than double, a fit carried to cold it was never fitted on.

`Ruling:`

### 92. The interconnection queue: Add the recent wait beside the all-years median

- **Page:** The interconnection queue, https://erw-flame.vercel.app/queues?grid=pjm
- **Question:** The headline "Request to operation" is the median wait over every year. The datacenter cost page's All grids tab gives another wait for the same grid: the median of the projects that switched on in 2023 to 2025. Should the headline carry that second wait beside the first?
- **Now:** One median over every year: Texas 3.70 years, California 6.02, PJM 3.15, the Southwest Power Pool 4.06, the Midwest 3.04, New York 4.61, the Southeast 3.79, the West 2.05.
- **Other choice:** A second figure in the headline's note, the projects that reached operation in 2023 to 2025: Texas 3.82 years (227 projects), California 8.33, PJM 5.94 (169 projects), the Southwest Power Pool 7.05, the Midwest 4.87, New York 7.27 (5 projects), the Southeast 4.97, the West 4.09. Each equals the All grids tab's figure.
- **Change:** One figure added for each of eight grids; the recent wait is longer in every grid (PJM +88.6 percent, the West +99.9 percent, Texas +3.3 percent).
- **Branch:** [wip/alt-queues-1](https://github.com/SamuelEnrique/erw/tree/wip/alt-queues-1)
- **Screenshots:** the other choice: [queues-1-recent-wait-1280.png](https://github.com/SamuelEnrique/erw/blob/main/docs/reviews/ruling-alternatives/queues-1-recent-wait-1280.png); the page today: screenshot not taken yet
- **Recommendation:** **no basis to recommend**. No allowed ground decides it. To weigh: two pages of the site now give different waits for one grid (PJM 3.15 years here, 5.94 on the All grids tab), each right for its own set of projects; the second figure lets a reader see both and why they differ.
- **Figures from:** [the measurement, interconnection queue](https://github.com/SamuelEnrique/erw/blob/main/docs/reviews/ruling-impact-queues-2026-11-17.md), [the record of the branches, interconnection queue](https://github.com/SamuelEnrique/erw/blob/main/docs/reviews/ruling-alternatives-queues-2026-11-17.md)
- **Evidence (looked up on 19 November 2026):**
  - **Published practice:** Berkeley Lab, the source of the page's figures, never gives one median over every year; it gives the wait by the year a project came online and leads with the newest. "Queued Up: 2026 Edition" (the slide deck, https://eta-publications.lbl.gov/sites/default/files/2026-06/queued_up_2026_edition.pdf): slide 4, "the median project built in 2025" took 61 months from request to operation; slide 51 charts the median by operation year, with each region's number of projects by year; the summary on slide 55 gives under 2 years for projects built in 2004 to 2008, over 4 for 2019 to 2024 and 5 for 2025. The edition's page (https://eta-publications.lbl.gov/publications/queued-2026-edition-characteristics) says the same of projects built in 2025. Berkeley Lab uses the newest single year; the three years 2023 to 2025 pooled are the ERW's All grids tab's choice. No second publisher was read today.
  - **Samuel's own rulings on a like question:** The page standard, rule 9 of the chain file of 16 November 2026 (`queue/done/302-day-16-november-oldlaptop-large-load-on-the-site.md`): two cases side by side wherever a reader would compare, and an upgrade "drops nothing the page had"; the other choice adds the recent wait and keeps the all-years median. The same file's goal 4 asks that a reader get the time to connect "without a figure that contradicts another page"; today PJM reads 3.15 years here and 5.94 on the All grids tab. No ruling names the queue page's headline (searched the two rulings files and the roadmap's rulings for wait, median, queue and operation).
  - **The tool's own method note:** `docs/methods/interconnection_queue_summary.md`, "The rows", holds both: the median over every dated request of the file, and the median by operation year, written from five dated requests. `docs/methods/interconnection_explorer.md`, "Whose wait", names the three waits the site gives for one queue (by entry year, over all years on `/queues`, and the last three operation years on the All grids tab) and says they do not give one number "and are not meant to". Neither note says which of them the headline of `/queues` carries.
- **Recommendation after the evidence:** **switch**. Berkeley Lab's practice decided it: the publisher of these figures states the wait for the projects that came online most recently and gives no all-years median at all, so the recent wait belongs beside the headline, and Samuel's page standard (two cases side by side, nothing dropped) fits adding it while the all-years median stays.

`Ruling:`

- **Page:** Demand growth, Texas, https://erw-flame.vercel.app/demand?grid=ercot
- **Question:** Should the page add a section on growth inside a grid, zone by zone?
- **Now:** Not drawn.
- **Other choice:** Texas's load zones, average load in 2019 against 2025: Far West 3,435 to 7,478 MW (+117.7 percent), North 854 to 1,689 (+97.8), South Central 6,970 to 8,877 (+27.4), Coast 12,354 to 14,745 (+19.4), North Central 13,819 to 15,307 (+10.8), West 1,292 to 1,384 (+7.2); the whole grid 43,817 to 55,733 (+27.2). The zones of the held grids are not read.
- **Change:** A section of seven rows added; none that stands moves.
- **Branch:** [wip/alt-demand-3](https://github.com/SamuelEnrique/erw/tree/wip/alt-demand-3)
- **Screenshots:** the other choice: [demand-3-1280.png](https://github.com/SamuelEnrique/erw/blob/main/docs/reviews/ruling-alternatives/demand-3-1280.png); the page today: [demand-3-default-1280.png](https://github.com/SamuelEnrique/erw/blob/main/docs/reviews/ruling-alternatives/demand-3-default-1280.png)
- **Recommendation:** **no basis to recommend**. No allowed ground decides it. To weigh: the zones show where the growth is (Far West has more than doubled); only Texas's zones are public and held, so the section would exist for one grid.
- **Figures from:** [the measurement, demand growth](https://github.com/SamuelEnrique/erw/blob/main/docs/reviews/ruling-impact-demand-2026-11-17.md), [the record of the branches, demand growth](https://github.com/SamuelEnrique/erw/blob/main/docs/reviews/ruling-alternatives-demand-2026-11-17.md)
- **Evidence (looked up on 19 November 2026):**
  - **Published practice:** Two publishers give a grid's demand by zone; neither prints a zone's growth since a base year as a table, and none says where a third party's page should carry it. EIA, Today in Energy, "EIA launches redesigned Hourly Electric Grid Monitor with new data and functionality" (21 August 2019, https://www.eia.gov/todayinenergy/detail.php?id=40993): "eight balancing authorities report subregional demand data", ERCOT, PJM, the Southwest Power Pool and California's among them, so zone demand is public for more grids than Texas. ERCOT, "2025 Long-Term Hourly Peak Demand and Energy Forecast" (8 April 2025, https://www.ercot.com/files/docs/2025/04/08/ERCOT-2025-Long-Term-Load-Forecast-Report.pdf), pages 2 and 12: ERCOT models and forecasts each of its eight weather zones on its own and sums them to the grid.
  - **Samuel's own rulings on a like question:** None found on a zone section for the demand page (searched `docs/state/rulings_2026-10-11.md`, `docs/state/rulings_2026-11-06.md`, the roadmap's "Rulings" and "Holds", and the page standard for: zone, sub-region, demand growth, section). The page standard of 16 November 2026 says an upgrade is made to the page itself and drops nothing, which the other choice respects, and does not say what a page must add. What a page is for is his call.
  - **The tool's own method note:** `docs/methods/demand_growth.md`, "What the tool does not hold": the sub-regions and the planning areas are not on this page; they have their own tables and notes. `docs/methods/subregion_demand_growth.md`: the warehouse already holds a zone's own growth from EIA's sub-regions (UTC years, each year on the year before), and the All grids tab of the datacenter cost page reads it for a row whose zone EIA names. `docs/methods/ercot_zone_load_hourly.md`: the table the other choice reads holds ERCOT's eight weather zones, "not its load zones or hubs". So the site would carry zone growth from two sources on two pages, over different years, and the section as built names six zones as load zones.
- **Recommendation after the evidence:** **no basis to recommend**. Looked for and not found: a ruling or a method rule on whether the demand page carries a section that exists for one grid; the publishers show only that zone demand is a figure they give, and a ruling would also have to say which of the two zone sources the site already holds the section stands on.

- **Question:** A grid's net imports of power are on the Grid page, and the supply page only links there. Should the supply page draw each grid's net imports for the newest month?
- **Now:** A link to the Grid page; no net import figure here.
- **Other choice:** One line with seven figures. October 2026, by EIA's hourly exchange data: California 2,831 GWh (3,932 MW on average), New York 1,377 GWh, the Midwest 1,217 GWh, Texas 66 GWh (29 days held), New England -23 GWh, the Southwest Power Pool -344 GWh, PJM -359 GWh (the last three are net exporters).

- **Screenshots:** screenshot not taken yet
- **Recommendation:** **no basis to recommend**. No allowed ground decides it. To weigh: the figures are EIA's public data and already on another page of the site, so the gain is one click saved.
- **Figures from:** [the measurement, supply and trade](https://github.com/SamuelEnrique/erw/blob/main/docs/reviews/ruling-impact-supply-2026-11-17.md), [the record of the branches, supply and trade](https://github.com/SamuelEnrique/erw/blob/main/docs/reviews/ruling-alternatives-supply-2026-11-17.md)
- **Evidence (looked up on 19 November 2026):**
  - **Published practice:** EIA does it both ways in its own products, so practice does not decide. EIA, Frequently Asked Questions, "Does EIA publish data on peak or hourly electricity generation, demand, and prices?" (https://www.eia.gov/tools/faqs/faq.php?id=100&t=3): in the Hourly Electric Grid Monitor, "Electricity supply includes" net generation and the interchange between regions, so a grid's exchange is drawn with its supply there. EIA, Electric Power Monthly, Table ES1.A (https://www.eia.gov/electricity/monthly/epm_table_grapher.php?t=table_es1a): the monthly summary of supply by fuel has no row of net imports. No operator's page that sets net imports beside fuel supply was read today.
  - **Samuel's own rulings on a like question:** None found on drawing on one page a figure another page of the site already draws (searched `docs/state/rulings_2026-10-11.md`, `docs/state/rulings_2026-11-06.md`, the roadmap's "Rulings" and the page standard for: net imports, supply, link, exchange, two pages). The page standard of 16 November 2026 asks that every figure carry its source and that nothing be dropped; it does not say whether a figure is drawn once or twice. Bearing on three of the seven figures: the hold on New York's, New England's and the Midwest's figures, and the open questions on drawing EIA's public figures for those grids (entries 71 and 72 of this list), which are his to rule first.
  - **The tool's own method note:** `docs/methods/supply_and_trade.md` gives no rule on a grid's exchange with its neighbours: the page holds the country's trade in crude, products and gas and the fuel burned for power. The table the other choice reads is described under the Grid page's note (`docs/methods/grid_network.md`, "Who supplies a grid"; `docs/methods/ba_supply_monthly.md`). The read that raised the question (`docs/reviews/supply-read-2026-11-16.md`, row 20) took the default: linked, not drawn here.
- **Recommendation after the evidence:** **no basis to recommend**. Looked for and not found: a ruling or a method rule on whether a figure already on the Grid page is drawn again on the supply page; EIA counts interchange in supply on its monitor and leaves it out of its monthly supply summary, and three of the seven figures wait on the hold on three grids.

### 98. Power prices, a hub's page: Split a hub's table into on-peak and off-peak

- **Page:** Power prices, a hub's page, https://erw-flame.vercel.app/prices
- **Question:** A hub's table gives one average, low and high over all hours. The price board splits prices into on-peak hours (6 a.m. to 10 p.m. on weekdays) and off-peak. Should a hub's page show the same split?
- **Now:** One row over all hours of the last 30 days. Day-ahead: the Southwest Power Pool's North hub USD 30.29 per MWh, its South hub 22.85, Texas's Houston hub 36.15, Southern California 41.95.
- **Other choice:** The all-hours row stays, followed by an on-peak and an off-peak row. North hub 39.10 on-peak and 20.79 off-peak; South hub 30.23 and 14.91; Houston 39.42 and 32.56; Southern California 37.31 and 47.04 (with its solar, on-peak is the cheaper).
- **Change:** Two rows added to each market; the on-peak figure is up to +32.3 percent and the off-peak up to -34.7 percent against the all-hours average.
- **Branch:** [wip/alt-prices-1](https://github.com/SamuelEnrique/erw/tree/wip/alt-prices-1)
- **Screenshots:** screenshot not taken yet
- **Recommendation:** **no basis to recommend**. No allowed ground decides it. To weigh: the board and the hub pages would then cut the day the same way; public holidays are not taken out of the on-peak hours.
- **Figures from:** [the measurement, power prices](https://github.com/SamuelEnrique/erw/blob/main/docs/reviews/ruling-impact-prices-2026-11-17.md), [the record of the branches, power prices](https://github.com/SamuelEnrique/erw/blob/main/docs/reviews/ruling-alternatives-prices-2026-11-17.md)
- **Evidence (looked up on 19 November 2026):**
  - **Published practice:** The two publishers read both report hub and zone prices by the peak block. The Independent Market Monitor for ERCOT (Potomac Economics), "2021 State of the Market Report for the ERCOT Electricity Markets" (https://www.potomaceconomics.com/wp-content/uploads/2022/05/2021-State-of-the-Market-Report.pdf), Appendix, "Real-Time Market Prices" and Figure A1, PDF pages 157 and 158 of 211: average prices for each month in two blocks, Peak (hours ending 7 to 22 on weekdays) and Off-Peak (every other hour), chosen because they "align with the categories traded in forward markets". EIA, "Wholesale Electricity and Natural Gas Market Data" (https://www.eia.gov/electricity/wholesale/): every power product it republishes from ICE is a peak product, none an all-hours average. The exchanges' own product definitions were not read today (ICE's terms of use bar robots; CME's site did not answer; NERC's robots file refuses Anthropic's agents), so the holiday list and the western Saturday are not confirmed from them today.
  - **Samuel's own rulings on a like question:** None found on a hub page's split (searched the two rulings files, the roadmap and the page standard for peak and off-peak). The page standard: an upgrade "drops nothing the page had" (the all-hours row stays under the other choice).
  - **The tool's own method note:** `docs/methods/price_board.md`, "price_board_peak_offpeak" and line 2 of "The decisions on the page": on-peak is the hours ending 7 to 22 local, Monday to Friday, California Monday to Saturday, with NERC's six holidays off-peak all day; the board draws the split for each grid's main hub only, so Houston, West, the pool's South hub and Northern California have it on no page. `docs/methods/power_prices.md` has no rule on a split. The branch as built does not yet cut the day as the board's note does: it takes Monday to Friday for every grid, California too, and leaves the holidays in the on-peak hours (the 30 days measured hold Veterans Day, which is not one of the six; Thanksgiving is the next that is).
- **Recommendation after the evidence:** **switch**. Published practice decided it: ERCOT's market monitor and EIA's exchange data both give hub prices by the peak block, and the board's method note already fixes the hours, which the branch must follow (the holidays, California's Saturday) before it is the same split.

`Ruling:`

### 105. Power prices, a hub's page: Add the newest whole month to a hub's page

- **Page:** Power prices, a hub's page, https://erw-flame.vercel.app/prices
- **Question:** A hub's page shows the last 30 days as they roll; whole calendar months are on the comparison page only. Should a hub's page add a line for the newest whole month?
- **Now:** The rolling 30 days only. Real time: the Southwest Power Pool's North hub USD 26.13 per MWh, Southern California 41.79. Day-ahead: Southern California 41.95.
- **Other choice:** A row for the newest whole month under them. October 2026: the North hub real time 33.56, its South hub 25.31, Texas North 33.86, Southern California 43.94; day-ahead Southern California 46.78.
- **Change:** One row added to each market; October against the rolling 30 days: North hub +28.5 percent, Southern California day-ahead +11.5 percent.
- **Branch:** [wip/alt-prices-2](https://github.com/SamuelEnrique/erw/tree/wip/alt-prices-2)
- **Screenshots:** screenshot not taken yet
- **Recommendation:** **no basis to recommend**. No allowed ground decides it. To weigh: a month can be read whole without leaving the page; the rolling figure and the month's differ by up to 28.5 percent, which a reader may take for a disagreement unless each is labelled.
- **Figures from:** [the measurement, power prices](https://github.com/SamuelEnrique/erw/blob/main/docs/reviews/ruling-impact-prices-2026-11-17.md), [the record of the branches, power prices](https://github.com/SamuelEnrique/erw/blob/main/docs/reviews/ruling-alternatives-prices-2026-11-17.md)
- **Evidence (looked up on 19 November 2026):**
  - **Published practice:** The publishers read give hub and zone prices by whole calendar months and years, and none gives a rolling 30 days; but where on a site a month's figure sits is not a choice they make. The Independent Market Monitor for ERCOT (Potomac Economics), "2021 State of the Market Report for the ERCOT Electricity Markets" (https://www.potomaceconomics.com/wp-content/uploads/2022/05/2021-State-of-the-Market-Report.pdf): monthly averages (Figure A1, PDF pages 157 and 158 of 211) and yearly ones (Table 1, PDF page 44). EIA, "Wholesale Electricity and Natural Gas Market Data" (https://www.eia.gov/electricity/wholesale/): files by calendar year and trade date. The operators' own monthly reports were not read today (ERCOT's site could not be reached; California's operator was not asked for one).
  - **Samuel's own rulings on a like question:** None found on a month's line (searched the two rulings files, the roadmap and the page standard for month, rolling and 30 days). Near it: the roadmap's line of 12 October 2026 that the battery headline counts whole months only (a headline of another tool), and the page standard's "drops nothing the page had" (the rolling rows stay under the other choice).
  - **The tool's own method note:** `docs/methods/power_prices.md`, "A hub's page": the page is the hub's last 30 days; the read behind it (`docs/reviews/prices-read-2026-11-16.md`, rows 8 and 10) put the whole month on `/prices/compare`, linked from the first tab since then, and left a month's line here as a default for this ruling. `docs/methods/hub_price_comparison.md`, "The two windows": the last whole month of every hub and zone is that page's figure. So the month's figure is already on the site, one link away, and the question is where it stands, not whether a reader can have it. The browser read of the branch found the pool's real-time month line blank until its real-time table is named (entry 44).
- **Recommendation after the evidence:** **no basis to recommend**. The publishers' whole months show the unit a reader would compare with, but the site already gives every hub's newest whole month on the comparison page, and no ruling, standard or note says it must also stand on the hub's page.

`Ruling:`

### 110. The demand growth explorer: An input: a site's megawatts as a share of the grid's growth

- **Page:** The demand growth explorer, https://erw-flame.vercel.app/explore/demand-growth
- **Question:** Should a reader be able to type a site's size in megawatts and see it as a share of each grid's growth since 2019?
- **Now:** No input.
- **Other choice:** A section with one input. Average demand in 2025 less 2019: Texas +11,920 MW (a 300 MW site is 2.52 percent of it, a 1,000 MW site 8.39); PJM +4,903 MW (6.12 and 20.39); the Southwest Power Pool +3,427 (8.75 and 29.18); the Midwest +1,633 (18.37 and 61.24); California +1,089 (27.54 and 91.80); the Lower 48 +32,220 (0.93 and 3.10). New York and New England fell, so they read "demand fell: no share".
- **Change:** An input and a figure for each grid added; no figure that stands moves.
- **Branch:** [wip/alt-demand-growth-3](https://github.com/SamuelEnrique/erw/tree/wip/alt-demand-growth-3)
- **Screenshots:** screenshot not taken yet
- **Recommendation:** **no basis to recommend**. No allowed ground decides it. To weigh: it answers a siting reader's first question in one number; the share compares a site's full size with a change in average demand, which a reader may take for more than it is.
- **Figures from:** [the measurement, demand growth explorer](https://github.com/SamuelEnrique/erw/blob/main/docs/reviews/ruling-impact-demand-growth-2026-11-17.md), [the record of the branches, demand growth explorer](https://github.com/SamuelEnrique/erw/blob/main/docs/reviews/ruling-alternatives-demand-growth-2026-11-17.md)
- **Evidence (looked up on 19 November 2026):**
  - **Published practice:** The publishers do not make this choice: none read today sets a site's megawatts against a grid's growth as a share. EIA's monitor and monthly tables give demand and its change (Electric Power Monthly, Tables ES1.A and 5.1, https://www.eia.gov/electricity/monthly/epm_table_grapher.php?t=table_es1a); ERCOT, "2025 Long-Term Hourly Peak Demand and Energy Forecast" (8 April 2025, https://www.ercot.com/files/docs/2025/04/08/ERCOT-2025-Long-Term-Load-Forecast-Report.pdf), the section on large loads (pages 8 and 9): large loads are projected in megawatts, with adjustment factors taken from what recent projects were observed to do; a site is not shown as a percent of past growth.
  - **Samuel's own rulings on a like question:** None found on a reader's input in an explorer (searched `docs/state/rulings_2026-10-11.md`, `docs/state/rulings_2026-11-06.md`, the roadmap's "Rulings" and the page standard for: input, site, share, explorer, megawatts). Near it, and pulling both ways: the roadmap ruling of 12 October 2026 that assumptions are editable in place (made for the cost tools, which compute), and the page standard of 16 November 2026, by which the tool "states facts and takes no view". Whether an explorer becomes a siting calculator is a question of what the page is for, which is his call alone.
  - **The tool's own method note:** `docs/methods/demand_growth.md`, the explorer's section: the page "computes nothing", every figure is one row of the table drawn with that row's check key, and its decision `as-written` reads "no arithmetic"; because of that, the note says, no choice on the page is editable in place. The other choice would be the page's first figure computed from what a reader types. The note also holds the caution the list gives: the growth is a change in average demand, and a site's megawatts are its full size.
- **Recommendation after the evidence:** **no basis to recommend**. Looked for and not found: a publisher that gives a site as a share of a grid's growth, or a ruling on a reader's input in an explorer; the method note says the explorer computes nothing, which describes the page as it is, and turning it into a calculator is a question of what the page is for, Samuel's alone.

`Ruling:`

- **Recommendation:** **no basis to recommend**. No allowed ground decides it. To weigh: the figures are in the page's own table with their source; under the present hold they too would read "license needed" until the question on EIA's figures above is ruled.
- **Figures from:** [the measurement, event pages](https://github.com/SamuelEnrique/erw/blob/main/docs/reviews/ruling-impact-events-2026-11-17.md), [the record of the branches, event pages](https://github.com/SamuelEnrique/erw/blob/main/docs/reviews/ruling-alternatives-events-2026-11-17.md)
- **Evidence (looked up on 19 November 2026):**
  - **Published practice:** The two accounts read describe this heat in words and print no airport's figure. EIA, "California consumers respond to appeals for electricity conservation during heat wave", Today in Energy, 28 September 2022 (https://www.eia.gov/todayinenergy/detail.php?id=54039): an "extreme heat wave" in words, with the demand figure and no temperature. NOAA NCEI, "Assessing the U.S. Climate in September 2022" (https://www.ncei.noaa.gov/news/national-climate-202209): the West's records in words and counts, no figure for Sacramento or Los Angeles. NOAA's own daily record for the two airports, read today (NCEI, Daily Summaries, https://www.ncei.noaa.gov/access/services/data/v1, stations USW00023232 Sacramento airport and USW00023174 Los Angeles International, 31 August to 9 September 2022, daily maximum): Sacramento 114 F on 5 September and 114 F again on 6 September; Los Angeles 102 F on 9 September (100 F on 4 September). The branch's figures are the highest hourly reading of the event table's rows: Sacramento 114.08 F on 5 September, which agrees but hides that the 6th tied it, and Los Angeles 100.94 F on 9 September, the right day and 1.06 F under NOAA's daily maximum. California's operator's own report on that month was not read (the file is over the reader's size limit).
  - **Samuel's own rulings on a like question:** None found on a temperature figure for this page (searched the two rulings files, the roadmap and the page standard for temperature, hottest and airport). The page standard: "The tool states facts", and every figure carries its source document. Under the hold of 21 October 2026 the two figures would read "license needed" in any case, as the record of the branch says, until the question on EIA's figures (entry 71) is ruled.
  - **The tool's own method note:** `docs/methods/events.md`, "Session 58" and line 7 of "The decisions on the pages": the page's weather is at Sacramento and Los Angeles from NOAA's hourly file, used for the degree days of the estimate with temperature; a figure is the row its sentence's rule picks, the earlier day where two are equal. `docs/methods/event_study.md`: a station's day is built from its hourly readings. The notes define no "hottest day" figure, and an hourly reading is not the day's recorded maximum.
- **Recommendation after the evidence:** **keep**. NOAA's own daily record decided it: read today it gives Los Angeles 102 F, not 100.94, and Sacramento 114 F on two days, not one, so the two figures as built are hourly readings that would be printed as each airport's hottest day, while EIA's and NOAA's own accounts give the heat in words as the page does.

Texas West less Houston over the last 30 days: real time (598 hours) an average of +1.14 USD per MWh, a median of 0.00, West below Houston in 297 hours; day-ahead (672 hours) an average of +0.14, a median of +1.22, from -51.48 to +50.08.

- **Figures from:** [the measurement, power prices](https://github.com/SamuelEnrique/erw/blob/main/docs/reviews/ruling-impact-prices-2026-11-17.md), [the record of the branches, power prices](https://github.com/SamuelEnrique/erw/blob/main/docs/reviews/ruling-alternatives-prices-2026-11-17.md)

The Independent Market Monitor for ERCOT (Potomac Economics), "2021 State of the Market Report for the ERCOT Electricity Markets" (https://www.potomaceconomics.com/wp-content/uploads/2022/05/2021-State-of-the-Market-Report.pdf), "Zonal Average Energy Prices in 2021" and Table 1, PDF page 44 of 211: Houston, North, South and West in one table, year by year, with the reason they differ (the cost of congestion as power is delivered over the network). EIA's wholesale page (https://www.eia.gov/electricity/wholesale/) lists hubs one by one and compares none. The exchanges' spread products were not read today (ICE's terms bar robots; CME's site did not answer).
  - **Samuel's own rulings on a like question:** The page standard, his standing rules (rule 9 of `queue/done/302-day-16-november-oldlaptop-large-load-on-the-site.md`): "Two cases side by side (A and B) wherever a reader would compare." A hub's page has one hub and no second case; the prices read of 16 November (`docs/reviews/prices-read-2026-11-16.md`, row 11) found that West less Houston is on no page. The same standard also says "No sideways scroll at 390 px", and the browser read of the branch found the page with a second hub 504 px wide at 390 (`docs/reviews/ruling-alternatives-prices-2026-11-17.md`, browser row 362): a fault to mend before it lands, not a reason against the A and B.
  - **The tool's own method note:** `docs/methods/power_prices.md`, "A hub's page": the page already gives real time less day-ahead over the hours both hold, the same form of figure for two markets of one hub; it has no rule on two hubs. `docs/methods/price_board.md`, "The groups", "Hub spreads": the board draws West less North, North less Houston and South less Houston as daily means, so a spread between two hubs of a grid is already a figure of the site, by the day and not by the hour. The foot's disagreement is one value, 407.815 USD per MWh, rounded two ways (407.81 and 407.82), not two figures.
- **Recommendation after the evidence:** **switch**. Samuel's page standard decided it: two cases side by side wherever a reader would compare, and a hub's page has no second hub today; the branch's width at 390 px must be mended first under the same standard.

### 116. What is on an electricity bill, the San Diego card: A climate credit box for San Diego, as the other two have

- **Page:** What is on an electricity bill, the San Diego card, https://erw-flame.vercel.app/learn/bill
- **Question:** The Pacific Gas and Electric and Southern California Edison bills have a box for the state's climate credit. San Diego's has none. Should it have one?
- **Now:** No box: San Diego's credit amount is not in the page's rules file.
- **Other choice:** A box. For scale the file holds Pacific Gas and Electric's credit at USD 36.18 and Edison's at USD 36.00; in a credit month San Diego's total would fall by its own amount.
- **Change:** One figure added; its amount is not held.
- **Branch:** not built: San Diego's credit amount is not in the rules file; its tariff sheet was not read.
- **Screenshots:** screenshot not taken yet
- **Recommendation:** **no basis to recommend**. No allowed ground decides it, and the figure is not held. To weigh: the three California bills would then be treated alike; it needs one tariff sheet read first.
- **Figures from:** [the measurement, electricity bill](https://github.com/SamuelEnrique/erw/blob/main/docs/reviews/ruling-impact-bill-2026-11-17.md), [the record of the branches, electricity bill](https://github.com/SamuelEnrique/erw/blob/main/docs/reviews/ruling-alternatives-bill-2026-11-17.md)
- **Evidence (looked up on 19 November 2026):**
  - **Published practice:** The regulator that sets the credit treats the three utilities alike and publishes San Diego's amount. California Public Utilities Commission, "California Climate Credit" (https://www.cpuc.ca.gov/climatecredit), the table of the residential electric credit by utility and year: for 2026 it gives Pacific Gas and Electric USD 36.18, Southern California Edison USD 36.00 and San Diego Gas and Electric USD 49.36, each paid on the bills of August and September. The first two are the figures the page's rules file already holds, to the cent. The Commission says residents receive the credit "on their electric and natural gas bills", San Diego's included. San Diego Gas and Electric's own tariff sheet was not read today (no sheet of its site was asked), so the Commission's page is the one source read for the amount.
  - **Samuel's own rulings on a like question:** None found on the San Diego card's credit (searched `docs/state/rulings_2026-10-11.md`, `docs/state/rulings_2026-11-06.md`, the roadmap's "Rulings" and the page standard for: climate credit, San Diego, bill, box). Bearing on how it is done, not on whether: the non-negotiables (`CLAUDE.md`, rules 1 and 5) and the page standard of 16 November 2026: real numbers only and every figure with its source document, so the box is added only with the amount read from its source and cited. Ruling 4 of 6 November 2026 (`docs/state/rulings_2026-11-06.md`): a tariff or a commission's record is a public regulatory record.
  - **The tool's own method note:** `docs/methods/bill.md`, "What the tool does not hold": San Diego's climate credit is listed among what was not read, beside its baseline allowances and season dates; the note gives no reason for leaving it out other than that the sheet was not read. The same note's arithmetic takes the credit "once, when chosen" for the other two utilities, and the box is off by default (the decision `climate-credit`), so a San Diego box would move no figure of the card as it opens.
- **Recommendation after the evidence:** **switch**. Published practice decides it: the Commission's own table gives the 2026 credit for all three utilities, San Diego's at USD 49.36 beside the two amounts the page already shows, so the only reason the method note gives for the missing box (the amount was not read) no longer holds once a session reads that amount into the rules file with its source.

### 117. What a datacenter pays, a flexible load in Texas: Add a way of running that avoids Texas's four summer peaks

- **Page:** What a datacenter pays, a flexible load in Texas, https://erw-flame.vercel.app/cost-of-power?grid=ercot&mw=300&run=hours
- **Question:** A large Texas customer's transmission charge is set by its demand in the grid's four summer peak intervals. Every way of running a flexible load on the page follows prices, and in summer 2025 those ways avoided none of the four peaks. Should the page add a way that switches off in the four peaks?
- **Now:** Price-following ways only: USD 0.00 per kW of the four-peak charge avoided in summer 2025.
- **Other choice:** A line beside them: a 300 MW load off in all four peak intervals of summer 2025 avoids the whole charge, USD 75.53 per kW a year at the 2026 rate: USD 22,658,181 a year for 300 MW. No forecast of the peaks is held, so the page says how many a forecast would have caught is not known.
- **Change:** One figure added: from USD 0 to as much as USD 22,658,181 a year for 300 MW.
- **Branch:** [wip/alt-cost-of-power-1](https://github.com/SamuelEnrique/erw/tree/wip/alt-cost-of-power-1)
- **Screenshots:** the other choice: [cost-of-power-1-fourth-way-1280.png](https://github.com/SamuelEnrique/erw/blob/main/docs/reviews/ruling-alternatives/cost-of-power-1-fourth-way-1280.png); the page today: screenshot not taken yet
- **Recommendation:** **no basis to recommend**. No recommendation: the measurement and the build give this figure differently (see the foot). To weigh: the figure is the most that could be saved with perfect knowledge of the peaks, and the page would have to say so, since no forecast is held.
- **Figures from:** [the measurement, datacenter cost](https://github.com/SamuelEnrique/erw/blob/main/docs/reviews/ruling-impact-cost-of-power-2026-11-17.md), [the record of the branches, datacenter cost](https://github.com/SamuelEnrique/erw/blob/main/docs/reviews/ruling-alternatives-cost-of-power-2026-11-17.md)
- **Evidence (looked up on 19 November 2026):**
  - **Published practice:** The one publisher read says this is the larger of the two ways Texas loads cut demand of their own accord. The Independent Market Monitor for ERCOT (Potomac Economics), "2021 State of the Market Report for the ERCOT Electricity Markets" (https://www.potomaceconomics.com/wp-content/uploads/2022/05/2021-State-of-the-Market-Report.pdf), "Self-dispatch", PDF page 68 of 211: of the two forms of voluntary response (a retailer's program, or cutting demand to avoid transmission cost), the second has the larger effect; that cost is shared out on the highest 15-minute load of each month from June to September, loads cut in the intervals that will probably be the peak, and by ERCOT's estimate "as much as 4,000 MW of load" was doing so in 2021. The Commission's rule (16 TAC 25.192) and ERCOT's own page on the four peaks were not read today (the Commission's site answered HTTP 402; ERCOT's site could not be reached), so this rests on one publisher, which cites ERCOT's own report.
  - **Samuel's own rulings on a like question:** Two, both recorded in `docs/methods/datacenter_pays_algorithm.md`, section 12. His ruling of 7 October 2026 (line 6): a flexible load follows a rule decided before each hour; the branch keeps to it by drawing a line beside the ways, not a fourth way to choose, since no forecast of the peaks is held. His ruling of 12 October 2026 (line 7, and `docs/roadmap.md`): the page gives an "if perfectly foreseen" figure beside the rule's own, so a figure that assumes the year is known, said as such, already stands on this page by his ruling.
  - **The tool's own method note:** The same note, line 19 and "Session 354": the four summer peaks are shown for the flexible way and not counted by default, at the Commission's newest approved rate and the newest summer held; a summer's avoided share is one less the mean of the load's weights in the four intervals. The price-following ways avoided none in summer 2025, so the page reads USD 0.00 per kW. The foot's disagreement is settled: the measurement's own next line and the build both give USD 22,658,181 a year for 300 MW at the 2026 rate (USD 75.527270 per kW) approved on 5 November 2026, which the page now reads. The figure is the most a load could avoid with the four intervals known, and the branch says how many a forecast would have caught is not known.
- **Recommendation after the evidence:** **switch**. The market monitor's report decided it: cutting load in the four peak intervals is the voluntary response it finds has the most effect, by as much as 4,000 MW in 2021, and Samuel's ruling of 12 October already lets this page give a figure for a perfectly foreseen year beside the rule's.

`Ruling:`

### 118. What a datacenter pays, California's connection steps: California: carry the earlier document's study length to the later one

- **Page:** What a datacenter pays, California's connection steps, https://erw-flame.vercel.app/cost-of-power?view=allgrids
- **Question:** California's grid operator wrote two documents on connecting large loads. The earlier one gives a study step a length; the later one's length is blank in the table. Should the earlier figure stand in for the later document?
- **Now:** Each as the table holds it: the earlier document's study step reads 90 days, the upper end of the "60 to 90 days" it prints; the later document's has no length, though it prints the same "60 to 90 days" on its page 25.
- **Other choice:** The later document's step reads the earlier one's figure, 90 days, marked as carried from the earlier document.
- **Change:** One cell gains a figure: 90 days, where the document itself prints 60 to 90 days.
- **Branch:** [wip/alt-cost-of-power-4](https://github.com/SamuelEnrique/erw/tree/wip/alt-cost-of-power-4)
- **Screenshots:** the other choice: [cost-of-power-4-caiso-steps-1280.png](https://github.com/SamuelEnrique/erw/blob/main/docs/reviews/ruling-alternatives/cost-of-power-4-caiso-steps-1280.png); the page today: screenshot not taken yet
- **Recommendation:** **no basis to recommend**. No recommendation: the measurement and the build give this figure differently (see the foot). To weigh: the build found that the later document itself prints "to be completed within 60 to 90 days" on its page 25, the blank is the table's own rule for a range (its method note writes a range with its words and no length) and the earlier row's 90 is the one written otherwise; one rule for both rows, asked of the data machine, would settle it without carrying anything.
- **Figures from:** [the measurement, datacenter cost](https://github.com/SamuelEnrique/erw/blob/main/docs/reviews/ruling-impact-cost-of-power-2026-11-17.md), [the record of the branches, datacenter cost](https://github.com/SamuelEnrique/erw/blob/main/docs/reviews/ruling-alternatives-cost-of-power-2026-11-17.md)
- **Evidence (looked up on 19 November 2026):**
  - **Published practice:** The publisher is the document itself, read today, and it states its own time. California ISO, "Large Load Enhancements, Draft Final Proposal", 24 September 2026 (https://stakeholdercenter.caiso.com/InitiativeDocuments/Draft-Final-Proposal-Large-Load-Enhancements-Sep-24-26.pdf), section A.3, "Large Load Interconnection Study Procedures", "Proposal", PDF page 25 of 71: as a minimum standard the operator expects the transmission owners' study procedures "to be completed within 60 to 90 days". So the later document is not silent: it gives a range, the same range the earlier document gave, and no single figure. The earlier document (the straw proposal of 11 August 2026) was not read again today.
  - **Samuel's own rulings on a like question:** None found on carrying a figure from one document to another (searched the two rulings files, the roadmap and the page standard for carry, stand in and straw). The standing rules that bear on it: the page standard's "Every figure carries its source document" and "Real numbers only, never a placeholder" (rule 9 of `queue/done/302-day-16-november-oldlaptop-large-load-on-the-site.md`), and `CLAUDE.md`, non-negotiable 5, every number traces to its source. A 90 drawn in the later document's row, marked as carried from the earlier one, would be a figure that row's own document does not print as a single number.
  - **The tool's own method note:** `docs/methods/large_load_connection_rules.md`, "What a row is" and "Chain 227: the CAISO's draft final proposal": a row holds what its own document states of its time; a range is written with its words and no duration; for this document "the range and the monthly review are written with their words and no duration", while the straw proposal's older row carries the range's upper end, 90 days, and "is left as it was written". So the blank is the table's rule for a range, not a reading error, and the page's own file (`site/data/siting/conn_rules.json`) already holds the later document's printed sentence with its page. The two rows differ only in how the same range was written down (the older row took its upper end); making them alike, and showing a row's printed words on hover, are the data machine's to settle and carry nothing.
- **Recommendation after the evidence:** **keep**. The method note decided it: a range is written with its words and no duration, and the later document, read today at its page 25, prints "60 to 90 days" itself, so there is nothing to carry and an earlier document's 90 would stand for a range the later one states in its own words.

`Ruling:`

- **Other choice:** The head's 6.23 percent alone; October's share and the "By day" line's share leave the view (the months' and the days' MWh stay). The branch as built takes out October's share only: the "By day" share is still drawn.

- **Figures from:** [the measurement, curtailment](https://github.com/SamuelEnrique/erw/blob/main/docs/reviews/ruling-impact-curtailment-2026-11-17.md), [the record of the branches, curtailment](https://github.com/SamuelEnrique/erw/blob/main/docs/reviews/ruling-alternatives-curtailment-2026-11-17.md)
- **Evidence (looked up on 19 November 2026):**
  - **Published practice:** One operator read, and its span is the calendar month. CAISO, "Managing the evolving grid" (https://www.caiso.com/about/our-business/managing-the-evolving-grid): the operator's standing chart is "Wind and solar curtailment totals by month", with a file of the monthly totals beside it. ERCOT publishes no curtailment figure at all (as `docs/methods/curtailment.md` records; ERCOT's Monthly Operational Overview of August 2026, read today, has none either), so no publisher gives Texas's share over any span; the market monitor's yearly report was not read today. A month's figure beside a figure for the days held is in line with the one operator read; no publisher was found that gives one span only.
  - **Samuel's own rulings on a like question:** The page standard (rule 9 of `queue/done/302-day-16-november-oldlaptop-large-load-on-the-site.md`, 16 November 2026): an upgrade is made to the page itself and "drops nothing the page had". The other choice takes October 2026's share off the view in three places. Nothing on Texas's spans in `docs/state/rulings_2026-10-11.md`, `docs/state/rulings_2026-11-06.md` or the roadmap's "Rulings" (searched: curtailment, span, share, limit, month).
  - **The tool's own method note:** `docs/methods/curtailment.md`, "Session 144: one page, every share" and "Texas: nine days, by the hour": the page was built to show every monthly share; for Texas a month is written when at least 95 percent of its hours are held, over the hours held, and the days carry their own "percent of the limit". The decisions on the page (number 3) give the share's unit as the month. So the month's share and the share over the whole days held are both figures the note says the page shows, each labelled with its days. On the count that kept the list from recommending: the build of the branch found two shares on the page (6.23 and 6.05 percent), not three; under either count the other choice removes a figure the note says the page shows.
- **Recommendation after the evidence:** **keep**. Samuel's page standard decides it (an upgrade drops nothing the page had), and the method note makes the month's share one of the figures the page was built to show, each share labelled with its days.

### 122. The February 2021 winter storm (Uri): One sentence on the price cap of February 2021

- **Page:** The February 2021 winter storm (Uri), https://erw-flame.vercel.app/events/uri-2021
- **Question:** The page gives the storm's highest price and says nothing of the price cap that held it there. Should it add one sentence on the cap?
- **Now:** No sentence on the cap.
- **Other choice:** One sentence: the Texas hub average stood at or above USD 8,900 per MWh in 345 fifteen-minute intervals, about 86 hours, from 15 to 19 February 2021. The cap's own figure (USD 9,000) is in a document the warehouse does not hold, so the sentence does not state it.
- **Change:** One figure added; nothing that stands moves.
- **Branch:** [wip/alt-events-4](https://github.com/SamuelEnrique/erw/tree/wip/alt-events-4)
- **Screenshots:** screenshot not taken yet
- **Recommendation:** **no basis to recommend**. No allowed ground decides it. To weigh: the 345 intervals are counted from rows the warehouse holds; the cap itself cannot be named until the commission's or the grid operator's document is read.
- **Figures from:** [the measurement, event pages](https://github.com/SamuelEnrique/erw/blob/main/docs/reviews/ruling-impact-events-2026-11-17.md), [the record of the branches, event pages](https://github.com/SamuelEnrique/erw/blob/main/docs/reviews/ruling-alternatives-events-2026-11-17.md)
- **Evidence (looked up on 19 November 2026):**
  - **Published practice:** The two publishers read both name the cap when they give the storm's prices. EIA, "Average Texas electricity prices were higher in February 2021 due to a severe winter storm", Today in Energy, 7 May 2021 (https://www.eia.gov/todayinenergy/detail.php?id=47876): wholesale prices stayed at or near the cap of USD 9,000 per MWh for about 77 hours, from midnight on 15 February to the morning of 19 February; and EIA's "Short-Term Energy Outlook Supplement: Sources of Price Volatility in the ERCOT Market", October 2022 (https://www.eia.gov/outlooks/steo/special/supplements/2022/2022_sp_03.pdf), PDF page 10 of 26: prices stayed at the cap for three days. The Independent Market Monitor for ERCOT (Potomac Economics), "2021 State of the Market Report for the ERCOT Electricity Markets" (https://www.potomaceconomics.com/wp-content/uploads/2022/05/2021-State-of-the-Market-Report.pdf), PDF pages 11 and 56 of 211: the cap and the value of lost load were both USD 9,000 per MWh, prices "were held" there through mid-morning on 19 February, and over 2021 the cap was reached in intervals adding up to more than 64 hours, with nearly 34 hours more between USD 7,000 and 8,999. The counts differ with the threshold and the series: the branch's own is 345 fifteen-minute intervals, about 86 hours, at or above USD 8,900 at the hub average. FERC's and NERC's inquiry report and the Commission's order were not read today (FERC's page answered HTTP 403 and the report's file is over the reader's size limit; the Commission's site answered HTTP 402; NERC's robots file refuses Anthropic's agents).
  - **Samuel's own rulings on a like question:** None found on this sentence (searched the two rulings files, the roadmap and the page standard for cap, Uri and 9,000). The page standard: "The tool states facts", and "Every figure carries its source document"; a published figure quoted with its document is how the event pages already give what the warehouse does not hold.
  - **The tool's own method note:** `docs/methods/events.md`, "What the tool does not hold": a figure the warehouse does not hold (generator outages, FERC's and ERCOT's) is "quoted where a page quotes" it and never computed. The branch counts its 345 intervals from rows held and leaves the cap's own figure out because no document giving it was held; the two EIA documents and the market monitor's report read today each print USD 9,000 and can be cited for it (EIA's are in the public domain).
- **Recommendation after the evidence:** **switch**. Published practice decided it: EIA and ERCOT's market monitor each name the USD 9,000 cap when they give the storm's prices, and with either cited the sentence can state the cap beside the count from the warehouse's own rows.
